<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Lunar Mansions (manazil al-qamar), which are the 28 stations the Moon passes through each month. After introducing the concept on the previous page, the text now details specific constellations and stars used for celestial navigation and seasonal timekeeping, moving through the stations of the Bull (Taurus) and the Giant (Orion).</meta>

<margin>
... the rising of the...
</margin>
A bright star of the second magnitude <note>original: "al-'adhama al-thaniya"; a historical way of classifying star brightness</note> is located on the right foot of Aquarius and is called **The Shepherd** <note>original: "al-Ra‘i"</note>; it sits upon the goad. Simultaneously, the "Shoulder of the Giant" <note>original: "Mankib al-Riya al-Jawzi"; referring to Betelgeuse in the constellation Orion</note> rises. These are two small stars with a distance of about a cubit <note>term: "cubit" (dhira‘); a traditional unit of angular measurement in the sky, roughly equal to 2 to 4 degrees</note> between them. From the rising point of Canopus <note>original: "Suhail"</note> emerges a star called **The Vizier** <note>original: "al-Wazar"</note>, accompanied by two small stars called **The Gift** <note>original: "Minna"</note>, which are considered the "eyes of the gathering."

<image-desc>A collection of red dots representing stars, arranged in four small clusters, with the names of the stars written above and below them in black ink: The Stranger (al-Warib), The Cauldron (al-Marjala), The Neck of the Earth ('Unuq al-Ard), and The Little Belly (al-Butayn).</image-desc>

<margin>
The rising of **The Little Belly** <note>original: "al-Butayn"</note> occurs on the sixteenth day of the first month of Tammuz <unclear>from Shamonad</unclear>.
</margin>

## The Pleiades
**The Pleiades** <note>original: "al-Thurayya"</note> rise as six stars close together, resembling a small cloud gathered in the shape of an isosceles triangle. From their position, the breadth and its bounty emerge. Then, it sets toward the north of the "Two Signs" <note>original: "al-Sharatayn"; Beta and Gamma Arietis</note> and **The Little Belly**. From the northern side, **Capella** <note>original: "al-‘Ayyuq"</note> rises with them; it is a bright star of the first magnitude. To its right is the "Path of the Crossing," except for six stars, two of which are small and called the "Foot of Capella." Along with it rises **The Hinderer** <note>original: "al-‘A’iq"</note>, a star not far from Orion. From the southern side, a cluster of stars called **The Cattle** <note>original: "al-Baqar"</note> rises. As for the "King of the Reins" <note>original: "Malik al-A‘inna"; the constellation Auriga</note>, it was given this name because it sits between the constellations of Taurus and Ursa Major. It rises from their vicinity toward the north and toward Leo. There is no northern figure behind it, so it serves as the northern figure and the figure of the Zodiac. This is their form, and the secret within them—and the great size of the stars within them—is a radiating brilliance. They represent the image of the Mansion and its "eyes."

<image-desc>A schematic diagram of the stars (The Pleiades and surrounding area) represented by red dots, with explanatory labels: The Butting (al-Nath), The Pleiades (al-Thurayya), The Hinderer (al-A'iq), The Museum (al-Mathaf), Capella (al-Ayyuq), and the Foot of Capella (Rijl al-Ayyuq).</image-desc>

<margin>
The Pleiades rise on the ninth day of Tammuz, which is the fourteenth of the...
</margin>

## Aldebaran
Then **Aldebaran** rises. It consists of seven stars: six forming the shape of the letter *Dal* <note>The Arabic letter د, which resembles a "V" shape</note> and one of them is great, being of the first magnitude. Its name is **The Eye of the Bull** <note>original: "‘Ayn al-Thawr"</note>. It is also called the Follower <note>original: "al-Tabi‘"</note> and is of the first magnitude. To its north are the "manger" and the "King." Many stars rise with it from the direction of the Levant <note>Syria/North</note> in a crooked formation called **The Hyades** <note>original: "al-Ghalabid," likely a variant of "al-Qala'is"</note>, which are scattered. From the south, stars rise in a row called **The Cattle**. At its rear is a "lamp" of third-magnitude stars. Between the Pleiades and Aldebaran are two stars that look like two triangles; they possess humility. The first part of Aldebaran to rise is the tip of the "Dal" shape, and the last to rise is the great star above the "Pillar of the King."

<image-desc>An illustration of the star Aldebaran (The Eye of the Bull) and the surrounding stars (The Hyades) represented by red dots, with labels: The Cattle (al-Baqar), Aldebaran, The Hyades (al-Falayid), and the Pillar of the King (Manar al-Malik).</image-desc>

<margin>
Aldebaran rises on the second day of August, which is the twenty-seventh of July.
</margin>

## Al-Haq‘ah
Then **Al-Haq‘ah** <note>term: "Al-Haq'ah"; the Three Stars in the head of Orion</note> rises, which is also called **Meissa** <note>original: "al-Maysan"</note>. It is the head of Orion <note>original: "al-Jawza"</note>. It consists of three stars on its side at the "Head of the Donkey," and the "Two Poet Stars" <note>original: "al-Kawkaban al-Sha‘iran"; Sirius and Procyon</note>. There are three small stars crossing them (seven in total), and the "Rowed Camel" is called **The Flight** <note>original: "al-Tayra"</note>. The two distant stars are its feet. The head is between the **Al-Haram** and **Al-Nakhid**, which incline toward the north. Al-Nakhid is a star of Orion of the second magnitude, while Al-Haram is a star of the first magnitude. Another star is near Al-Haram. Between Meissa and Aldebaran are stars called **The Effacer** <note>original: "al-Mahi"</note> at the head of Orion. Connected stars called **The Flags** <note>original: "al-A‘lam"</note> rise with them from the north. From the right side, circular stars rise called **The Cotton** <note>original: "al-Qattan"</note>, and below them are stars called **The Scratchers** <note>original: "al-Hikaki"</note>, which are two red stars resembling spots.

<vocab>**The Pleiades** (al-Thurayya), **Aldebaran** (al-Dabaran), **The Mane** (al-Haq'ah), **The Little Belly** (al-Butayn), **Meissa** (al-Maysan), **Orion** (al-Jawza), **Capella** (al-Ayyuq), **Lunar Mansions** (Manazil al-Qamar), **Seasonal Weather Signs** (al-Anwa'), **The Hyades** (al-Falayid/al-Qala'is).</vocab>

It includes diagrams of these star clusters to aid in identification for timekeeping and navigation.</summary>
<keywords>astronomy, Lunar Mansions, Pleiades, Aldebaran, Orion, star magnitudes, celestial navigation, Arabic constellations</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Lunar Mansions (manazil al-qamar). The previous page discussed the Pleiades and the earlier stations. Here, the author moves through the mansions of the Mark (al-Han‘ah), the Arm (al-Dhira‘), the Manger (al-Nathrah), and the Glance (al-Tarf), correlating them with the months of June and July and the constellations of the Giant (Orion), the Lion (Leo), and the Crab (Cancer).</meta>

<image-desc>
A celestial diagram consisting of several clusters of stars represented by red dots, each with accompanying labels.
- Top cluster: A semi-circular arrangement labeled "The Crown" <note>original: al-Taj</note> and "The Mark" <note>original: al-Haq'ah</note>. To the left are "The King's Saw" <note>original: Minshar al-Malik</note> and "The Flags" <note>original: al-A‘lam</note>. To the right are "Suhayl of the Ass" <note>original: Suhayl Himar</note>, "The Gifts" <note>original: al-‘Ataya</note>, "The Prostrate" <note>original: al-Mukaba</note>, and "The Helper" <note>original: al-Najid</note>. In the center is "The Pests" <note>original: al-Hawam</note>.
</image-desc>

<margin>
Eighteen days remain of it.
</margin>

<margin>
The rising of **The Mark** <note>original: al-Han‘ah; the 6th Lunar Mansion</note> occurs on the twenty-sixth day of June, which corresponds to the twentieth day of Haziran <note>Haziran: the Syriac name for June</note>.
</margin>

**The Mark** is so named because it consists of five stars in the shape of a scepter or crook, or perhaps because of what appears as the "Two Stars" <note>original: al-Najma</note> and the "Manuscript" <note>original: al-Mushaf</note>, as their curves extend toward the North and the West. Simultaneously, the "Extended Arm of the Lion" <note>original: Dhira‘ al-Asad al-Mabsutah</note> rises from the direction of the Levant <note>original: al-Sham; the North</note>. From the direction of Yemen <note>the South</note> rises "Suhayl of the Ass" <note>original: Suhayl al-Himar</note>, which is a bright star whose rising point is lower than that of Canopus <note>original: Suhayl al-Yamani</note>. It appears larger because Canopus is only partially visible at its first rising, which is why it is called the "Hidden One" <note>original: al-Muhbit</note>. At this time, "The Little Dog Star" <note>original: al-Shi‘ra al-Ghumaysa; Procyon</note> also rises. This is a tongue-shaped star that shone in the "frames of the Giant" <note>original: surar al-Jawza; Orion</note>. Another star is released like a "Sandgrouse" <note>original: Kudri</note> at the hand of the constellation, which is the seventh from the "Upper Tent" <note>original: al-Khayma al-A‘la</note>, the "Arm," and "The Omen" <note>original: al-‘Awf</note>. The fifth star sits lower than the "Arm," and the "Arm" itself consists of two stars. These are the forms of them all.

<image-desc>
A second celestial diagram with red dots. Labels include "The Beard" <note>original: al-Lihya</note>, "Leader of the Beard" <note>original: Qa’id al-Lihya</note>, "The Glove" <note>original: al-Qaffaz</note>, "The Foot of the Giant" <note>original: Rijl al-Jabbar; the star Rigel</note>, "Southern Suhayl" <note>original: Suhayl al-Yamani; Canopus</note>, and "The Two Ostriches" <note>original: al-Zaliman</note>.
</image-desc>

<margin>
The rising of **The Arm** <note>original: al-Dhira‘; the 7th Lunar Mansion</note> occurs on the ninth day of July, which is the ninth day of Tammuz <note>Tammuz: the Syriac name for July</note>.
</margin>

**The Arm** refers to the "Folded Arm" <note>original: al-Maqbudah</note>, which consists of eight stars, though only two are prominent, separated by the visual distance of a "cubit" <note>term: dhira‘; a traditional unit of angular measurement in the sky</note>. Rising with them from the North are the "Amani," the "Vessel" <note>original: al-Qadr</note>, and the "Ladle" <note>original: al-Maghrafa</note>. After fifteen days, the "Daughters of the Bier" <note>original: Banat Na‘sh; the Big Dipper</note> appear, specifically the "Lofty One" from the front. From the South, "The Star that Crossed" <note>original: al-Shi‘ra al-‘Abur; Sirius</note> rises. It is a star that shone in the "First Greatness" <note>original: al-‘azm al-awwal; likely referring to first-magnitude brightness</note>. Accompanied by it is a star called the "Roarer" <note>original: Mirzama</note>, also known as the "Dog of the Heat" <note>original: Kalb al-Harr</note>. The "Dogs" and the "Wolves" act as dogs at its rising. These are the forms of the "Chariot" <note>original: al-Markaba</note> and its surroundings.

<image-desc>
A third celestial diagram. Central label "The Folded Arm" <note>original: al-Dhira‘ al-Maqbudah</note> next to a line of dots. Other labels include "Head of the Bear," "The Bright Star" <note>original: al-Farqad</note>, "The Little Dog Star" <note>original: al-Shi‘ra al-Ghumaysa; Procyon</note>, "The Ostriches," "The Cows," and "The Sandgrouse" <note>original: al-Kudri</note>.
</image-desc>

<margin>
The rising of **The Manger** <note>original: al-Nathrah; the 8th Lunar Mansion</note> occurs on the twenty-second day of July, which is the tenth day of Tammuz <note>The text provides two calendar systems; the 10th of Tammuz in the Julian calendar often aligns with late July</note>.
</margin>

**The Manger** then rises; it is also called the "Crib" <note>original: al-Ma‘laf</note>. It is a nebulous, spreading celestial object that looks like a small cloud. It is a misty patch located on the chest of the Crab <note>original: al-Saratan; the constellation Cancer</note> between two stars known as the "Two Asses" <note>original: al-Himaran</note>. One is on the right of the Crab and the other is the left eye. The Manger is situated slightly to the south of them. Rising with it from the North is one of the five "Daughters of the Bier" <note>the Big Dipper</note>, and from the South, "The Virgins" <note>original: al-‘Adhara</note>, which are five stars. They are also known as the "One-Eyed Manger." These are the forms of them all.

<image-desc>
A fourth celestial diagram. Labels include "The Daughters of the Bier," "The Bed" <note>original: Sarir</note>, "The Feminine" <note>original: al-Mu’annath</note>, "The Manger" <note>original: al-Nathrah</note>, "Head of the Hydra" <note>original: Ra’s al-Shuja‘</note>, "The Star that Crossed" <note>original: al-Shi‘ra al-‘Abur; Sirius</note>, and "The Flags."
</image-desc>

<margin>
The rising of **The Glance** <note>original: al-Tarf; the 9th Lunar Mansion</note> occurs on the twenty-third day of July, which is the tenth day of Tammuz.
</margin>

**The Glance** then rises, consisting of two stars positioned horizontally at the edge of the southern eye of the Lion <note>original: al-Asad</note>. One of them is more luminous than the northern one. This concludes the instruction.

<vocab>al-Han‘ah (The Mark), al-Dhira‘ (The Arm), al-Nathrah (The Manger), al-Tarf (The Glance), al-Shi‘ra al-Ghumaysa (Procyon), al-Shi‘ra al-‘Abur (Sirius), Banat Na‘sh (The Big Dipper), al-Saratan (Cancer), Manazil al-Qamar (Lunar Mansions), ‘Ilm al-Falak (Astronomy), Haziran (June), Tammuz (July)</vocab>

<summary>This page describes four Lunar Mansions—al-Han‘ah, al-Dhira‘, al-Nathrah, and al-Tarf—detailing their star configurations, their positions relative to constellations like Leo and Orion, and their rising dates in the solar and Syriac calendars. It provides a unique synthesis of indigenous Arabian star lore with classical astronomical terminology.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Lunar Mansions, the 28 stations the moon passes through in its monthly cycle. Following the stations of early summer, the text now describes the mansions associated with the constellations of the Lion (Leo) and the Virgin (Virgo), marking the transition into late summer and early autumn. The author continues to use "Yemen" to indicate the South and "Levant" or "Sham" to indicate the North, reflecting a traditional geographical orientation.</meta>

The third <note>This refers to a sequence or a specific star within the previous mansion group</note> rises alongside the "Rear of the Daughters of the Bier" <note>original: Mu’akhkhar Banat Na‘sh; the stars at the back of the Big Dipper/Ursa Major</note> from the North. From the South, the "Land of Torment" <note>original: Baladat al-‘Adhab</note> appears, which consists of four stars arranged closely together. They rise over eight days upon the "Chest of the Greater Dog" <note>original: Sadr al-Kalb al-Akbar; the constellation Canis Major</note>, and their rising point is beneath "Sirius" <note>original: al-Shi'ra</note>. This is the illustration of their grouping:

<margin>These stars rise on the ninth of Tishrin <note>Tishrin usually refers to October/November, but the text here correlates it with the month of August</note>, which is the ninth day of August <note>original: Ab</note>.</margin>

# The Forehead
Then **The Forehead** <note>original: al-Jabhah; the 10th Lunar Mansion, part of Leo</note> rises, which consists of four stars positioned crosswise; two are bright and two are faint. This "Forehead" is also called the "Heart of the Lion" <note>original: Qalb al-Asad; the star Regulus</note>. Rising with it is "The Foal" <note>original: al-Hiwar</note>, which is one of the three stars of the "Daughters of the Bier" <note>Ursa Major</note>. From the direction of Yemen <note>the South</note>, a small star called "The Solitary One" <note>original: al-Fard; the star Alphard in the constellation Hydra</note> appears, belonging to the "Station of Gemini" <note>original: Manhal al-Jawza</note>. **Canopus** <note>original: Suhayl</note> also rises in the Levant, the Hejaz, and Iraq. Its rising in Egypt occurs with "The Two Ribs" <note>original: al-Khurtan</note>, as it does in the West <note>North Africa/Maghreb</note>. They call it the "Solitary One of Mani" <note>original: al-Fardamani</note>.

<margin>The Forehead rises on the twenty-first of Tishrin, which is the fourth day of September <note>original: Aylul</note>.</margin>

<image-desc>A schematic diagram for the stars of the Forehead, Canopus, and the Ship, represented by red dots with labels: The Ship, The Lakes, The Solitary One, The Forehead, and Canopus.</image-desc>

# The Two Ribs
These are the **"Two Turners"** <note>original: al-Sarfatan</note>, two bright stars located on the loins of the Lion. Rising with them from the North is "The Neck" <note>original: al-‘Anaq; the star Mizar</note> from the "Daughters of the Bier," accompanied by a small star clinging to it <note>This likely refers to Alcor</note>. From Yemen <note>the South</note>, "Ser Astiv" <note>unclear technical name</note> and the "Feet of the Yemenite Canopus" rise. These are their images:

<margin>The Two Ribs <note>reading "al-Khurtan" for "al-Barrah"</note> rise on the third of Tishrin, which is the eighth day of September.</margin>

<image-desc>A schematic diagram for the stars of the Two Ribs and Canopus, represented by red dots with labels: Canopus, The Feet of Canopus, The Tawaseen, The Two Ribs, The Alhan, and the Star of Nama.</image-desc>

# The Change
Then **The Change** <note>original: al-Sarfah; the 12th Lunar Mansion, marked by the star Denebola</note> rises, which is the "Tail of the Lion." It is a bright star of the first magnitude. Alongside it rises "The Northern Tress" <note>original: al-Hilbah; the constellation Coma Berenices</note>, which are faint stars resembling a mirror in shape, though wider and more obscure. "The Wanderer" <note>original: al-Ghadri</note> from the "Daughters of the Bier" also rises, being the smallest of them. From Yemen <note>the South</note> rises "The Bend" <note>original: al-Mun‘atif</note>, and its stars belong to the "Eight of the Henna." These are their images:

<margin>The Change rises on the twelfth of Tishrin, which is the ninth day of September.</margin>

<image-desc>A schematic diagram for the stars of the Change and the Tress, represented by red dots with labels: The Henna, The Change, The Lion’s Followers, The Tress, and The Wanderer.</image-desc>

# The Barker
Then **The Barker** <note>original: al-‘Awwa; the 13th Lunar Mansion, part of Virgo</note> rises. It is shaped like an inverted letter *Kaf* <note>the Arabic letter ك</note> and consists of five stars. The first to appear is its head, located toward the North and extending Southward, with a bright star at its "knee." Rising with it from the North are "The Followers" <note>original: al-Tawabi‘</note>, which are stars beneath "The Wanderer" of the "Daughters of the Bier" in the North. They are four stars shaped like a square chair.

<margin>The Barker rises on the sixth of Tishrin, which is the eighth day of September.</margin>

<image-desc>A schematic diagram for the stars of the Barker and the Flyer, represented by red dots with labels: The Flyer, The Throne, The Barker, and The Tress.</image-desc>

# The Uplifted One
Then the **Unarmed Uplifted One** <note>original: al-Simak al-A‘zal; the star Spica</note> rises. It is preceded by the "Spear-bearing Uplifted One" <note>original: al-Simak al-Ramih; the star Arcturus</note>. The "Unarmed Uplifted One" consists of eight stars; the southernmost is "The Runner" <note>original: al-Rakdu</note>. The four stars with it are shaped like a Balance <note>the constellation Libra</note>. This is called "The Ear of Grain" <note>original: al-Sunbulah; the constellation Virgo</note>. From the North, the "Spear-bearing Uplifted One" rises, consisting of five stars.

<meta>This page continues the catalog of the Lunar Mansions, focusing on the constellations that appear as summer transitions into autumn. The previous page discussed the "Forehead" (al-Jabhah) of the Lion. We now move into the mansions associated with the body and tail of Scorpio (al-'Aqrab) and the surrounding stars, maintaining the geographical orientation where the "Levant" represents the North and "Yemen" represents the South.</meta>

<margin>The eighth [mansion] falls on it, which is in October <note>original: Tishrin al-Awwal</note>.</margin>
<margin>Scorpio falls on the twenty-eighth of August <note>original: Ab</note>, which is the eighteenth of September <note>original: Aylul</note>.</margin>

# **Scorpio**

**Scorpio** <note>original: al-‘Aqrab; here referring to the stars at the front of the constellation</note> rises, consisting of three stars positioned at the head of the "Enemy of the Sky" according to the Arabs. Among them are the stars of its "Shoulder" <note>original: Kaftah</note>, the largest of which is of the fifth magnitude <note>original: al-‘azm al-khamis</note>, and its "eyes" are in the "Northern Bowl" <note>original: al-Fakah al-Shamiyyah; usually referring to Corona Borealis</note>. These are circular stars, appearing like a segment of a circle, called the "Flicking of the Nostrils" <note>original: Mas‘at al-Manakir</note> and also "The Necklace" <note>original: al-Qiladah</note>. Within it is a bright star of the second magnitude. It rises five nights after the sun, accompanied by two bright stars. This is the illustration of their grouping:

<image-desc>A constellation diagram composed of red dots representing the head of Scorpio and surrounding stars. Labels include: "The Ostriches" (al-Na‘ayim), "The Ostrich Nest" (Udha al-Na‘am), "The Unarmed Spica" (al-Simak al-A‘zal), "The Spear-bearing Arcturus" (al-Simak al-Ramih), "The Flag of the Spica" (Rayat al-Simak), "The Knights" (al-Fursan), "The Knots" (al-‘Uqud), "The Bowl" (al-Fakah), "The Necklace" (al-Qiladah), and a note stating "and there the dwellings are located" (wa thumma yaqa‘ al-masakin).</image-desc>

---

<margin>The Claws rise in fourteen days, which is the end of October <note>original: Tishrin al-Awwal</note>.</margin>

# **The Claws**

These are the **Two Claws** <note>original: al-Zubanan; the 16th Lunar Mansion, the claws of the Scorpion, now part of Libra</note> of Scorpio. They are two bright stars, separated in appearance by the length of a spear or slightly less. The southern one rises before the northern one, and both are of the second magnitude. The "eyes" of the Claws to the North are "The Returning Ones" <note>original: al-‘Awa’id</note>, and to the South are "The Altar" <note>original: Mansak</note> and "The Blessed Tree" <note>original: Tuba</note>. The "Marketman" <note>original: al-Suqani</note> rises with them, consisting of three mountain-like stars connected to one another, and the other rises from the "Draper" <note>original: al-Bazzar</note> with seven stars. This is the illustration of their grouping:

<image-desc>A constellation diagram for the lunar mansion "al-Zubanan" using red dots. Descriptive labels include: "The Large-bellied" (al-A‘jar), "The Claws" (al-Zubanan), "The Returning Ones" (al-‘Awa’id), "The Birthplace" (al-Mawlid), and "The Eyes of the Claw" (‘Uyun al-Zubana).</image-desc>

---

<margin>The Crown rises on the twenty-eighth of October, which is the first of November <note>original: Tishrin al-Thani</note>.</margin>

# **The Crown**

Then **The Crown** <note>original: al-Iklil; the 17th Lunar Mansion, the head of Scorpio</note> rises, which consists of five stars with bent tips, curving toward the North. The "Breath of Caution" <note>original: Nasmat al-Hidharah</note> is at its heart. The three middle stars are of the fourth magnitude, the central one being the brightest. Nearby are other small "mountain" stars, six in total in the atmosphere that "holds the flesh." From it rise "The Returning Ones" <note>original: al-‘Awa’id</note>, which are four stars in a square formation rising from the North <note>original: al-Sham</note>. The "Trumpet" <note>original: al-Shabur</note>, the "Stone-Lined Hole," and the "Hushand" also rise from the sun. This is the illustration of their grouping:

<image-desc>A constellation diagram for "al-Iklil" (The Crown) depicted with red dots in a semi-circular arrangement. Labels include: "Gemini/Orion" (al-Jawza), "The Crown" (al-Iklil), "The Second Millstone" (al-Arha al-Thani), "The Returning Ones" (al-‘Awa’id), and "The Faint One" (al-Kamid).</image-desc>

---

<margin>The Heart rises on the twenty-eighth day of November, which is the first of November <note>The text repeats Tishrin al-Thani; likely meaning the transition between the two months</note>.</margin>

# **The Heart**

Then **The Heart** <note>original: al-Qalb; the 18th Mansion, the star Antares</note> rises, which is a bright star of the third magnitude situated between two shining stars on the "Rug" <note>original: al-Bisat</note>. It is slightly outside of them, leaning toward the North. Their "eyes" are in the direction of the North <note>original: al-Sham</note> toward the "Alighting Vulture" <note>original: al-Nasr al-Waqi‘; the star Vega</note>. Its rising occurs after the Crown. With it rises the bright star at the head of the Serpent held by the "Serpent-Bearer" <note>original: al-Hawwa; the constellation Ophiuchus</note>. Along with the two Vultures, they are called "The Two Growlers" <note>original: al-Hararan</note>. When they rise, the air begins to circulate. From the "Preceding Leopard" <note>original: al-Namir al-Sabiq</note> rises another bright star and various others. This is their illustration:

<image-desc>A constellation diagram for "al-Qalb" (The Heart/Antares) shown with red dots. Key labels include: "The Pleiades" (al-Thurayya), "The Heart" (al-Qalb), "The Alighting Vulture" (al-Nasr al-Waqi‘), "The Two Growlers" (al-Hararan), "The Returning Ones" (al-‘Awa’id), and "The Neglected One" (al-Suha).</image-desc>

---

<margin>The Sting rises on the twenty-eighth day of November <note>original: Tishrin al-Thani</note>.</margin>

# **The Sting**

Then **The Sting** <note>original: al-Sawlah; the 19th Mansion, the stinger of Scorpio</note> rises, which consists of eleven stars. Two of them are curved at the tip of the tail—the tail of Scorpio. Within it is the point of the Sting. Its "helpers" are "The Knights" <note>original: al-Fawaris</note> and "The Follower" <note>original: al-Radif; often the star Deneb</note>, which is a bright star. From the direction of the South <note>original: al-Tayman</note> are "The Two Woodchats" <note>original: al-Sardan</note>, which are two shining stars. This is the illustration of their grouping:

<image-desc>A constellation diagram for "al-Shawla" (The Sting) represented by red dots. The Ostriches rise on the twenty-eighth of December <note>original: Kanun al-Awwal</note> and the month of Tir <note>original: Tir Mah; likely referring to a local variant of the Persian calendar</note>, which is the first day of the forty-day winter period <term>Marba'aniyah|The coldest forty days of winter, traditionally starting in late December</term>.

# **The Ostriches**

**The Ostriches** <note>original: al-Na‘ayim</note> consist of two groups: the "Returning" <note>original: al-Sadirah</note> and the "Arriving" <note>original: al-Waridah</note>. They are eight stars; the four leading stars toward the East are called the "Returning" or the "Passing," while the ones toward the West are called the "Arriving." Each group forms a different quadrilateral shape, resembling a goat. Among them is a star called **The Shepherd** <note>original: al-Ra'i</note> located to the North of them. **The Town** <note>original: al-Baldah</note> is called the "Arriving" group here. Along with them rise **The Riders** <note>original: al-Fawaris</note>, three bright stars lined up. They cross the prohibited area <note>original: al-Haram</note>. From them rises **The Tower** <note>original: al-Barju</note>, a bright star also called "The Pride of the Craftsmen" <note>original: Fakhr al-Suna‘</note>. From the direction of Yemen <note>the South</note> rises **The Monkey** <note>original: al-Qird</note>, and in this period... <unclear>illegible</unclear>.

<margin>
The Town rises on the ninth of January <note>original: Kanun al-Thani</note>, which is the twenty-first of the month of Tir.

# **The Town**

**The Town** <note>original: al-Baldah</note> then rises. It is an empty space <note>this refers to a region in Sagittarius that lacks bright stars</note> situated in the middle of "The Birth" <note>original: al-Wiladah</note>. "The Birth" consists of six bright stars called **The Wings** <note>original: al-Ajniha</note>, and those hidden stars are called **The Obscure Ones** <note>original: al-A'ajim</note>. The bright ones rise shortly before Friday <note>possibly a temporal reference or a specific celestial alignment</note>. They are of the fourth magnitude <note>original: al-‘azm al-rabi‘</note>, while the "Enema" <note>original: al-Huqnah</note> is of the fifth magnitude. Some are to the North, and the arc of its bow is to the South. <unclear>tazu'ugha</unclear> there are water-drawers <note>original: sani</note>. From the South <note>Yemen</note> rise **The Two Little Gazelles** <note>original: al-Zaby al-Saghirah</note> and from the protection of the fever...

<image-desc>A cluster of red dots representing the lunar mansion The Town (Al-Balda). Labels: The Two Gazelles (al-Zabiyan), The Town (al-Balad), The Fish (al-Samakah), The Flying Eagle (al-Nasr al-Ta'ir / Altair).</image-desc>

<margin>
The Lucky Star of the Slaughterer rises on the twenty-sixth of January, which is the eighth of the month of Tir.
</margin>

# **The Lucky Star of the Slaughterer**

**The Lucky Star of the Slaughterer** <note>original: Sa'd al-Dhabih</note> then rises. It consists of two stars; two of them are bright, and one is hidden to the West-North. The Northern one is brighter than the Southern one. It is said that the hidden star beside it is its **Sheep** <note>original: Shatuhu</note>. Along with it rises **The Cluster** <note>original: al-'Anqud</note> from the North, which is the "Bench of the Crab" <note>original: Dast al-Saratan</note>, a group of clustered stars. From the direction of Yemen <note>the South</note> rise **The Offspring of the Ostrich** <note>original: Awlad al-Na'am</note>, which are faint stars. When the Lucky Star of the Slaughterer rises, it brings protection from the fever.

<margin>
The Lucky Star of the Devourer rises on the ninth of February <note>original: Shubat</note>, which is the twenty-first of the month of Tir.
</margin>

# **The Lucky Star of the Devourer**

**The Lucky Star of the Devourer** <note>original: Sa'd Bula'</note> then rises. These are two stars separated by a span. One is Western and the other is Eastern. The Western one is bright and the Eastern one is hidden. It is located in the "Wood-carrier" <note>original: al-Hattab</note>, North of a bright star. The **Shaft of the Cross** <note>original: 'Amud al-Salib</note> is beneath the Cross. In the South <note>Yemen</note> rises **The Lucky Star of the Spreader** <note>original: Sa'd Nashirah</note>, which are two very small stars.

<margin>
The Luckiest of the Lucky Stars rises on the twenty-second of February, which is the twelfth of the month of Tir.
</margin>

# **The Luckiest of the Lucky Stars**

**The Luckiest of the Lucky Stars** <note>original: Sa'd al-Su'ud</note> then rises. It consists of three stars in a specific shape. The Northern one is the brightest; the one below it to the South is less bright, and the other is hidden. The shaft of the Northern star is from the "Foremost Cup" <note>original: al-Qadh al-Muqaddam</note>. Along with it rises **The Camel's Hump** <note>original: Sanam al-Naqah</note>. From the South <note>Yemen</note> rises **The Lucky Star of the Two Desires** <note>original: Sa'd al-Hamma</note>, which are the two stars of Ramadan.

<image-desc>A cluster of red dots representing the lunar mansion The Luckiest of the Lucky Stars. Labels: The Luckiest of the Lucky Stars (Sa'd al-Su'ud), The Front of the Bucket (Muqaddam al-Dalw), The Camel's Hump (Sanam al-Naqah).</image-desc>

# **The Lucky Star of the Tents**

**The Lucky Star of the Tents** <note>original: Sa'd al-Akhbiya</note> then rises. It consists of two stars placed crosswise, perhaps with a third between them. One is Southern and the other is Northern, appearing in the North of Egypt.

<margin>The Water Bucket rises with it, as do the "tools," and it is the end of the mansion of Al-Ghafr <note>original: al-Ghafr; The Covering, a mansion in Virgo, though here referenced as a temporal or positional marker</note>.</margin>
The southern one is hidden, and below it to the south are stars the Arabs call **The Tents** <note>original: Akhbiya</note>. To the north is the **Barker** <note>original: ‘Awwa</note>, which is the neck of the Water Bucket. Rising with it are the **Depositors** <note>original: al-Wadiyat</note>, and among them are the two stars of **Raidan**. From the direction of Yemen <note>the South</note> is **The Excelling Auspicious Star** <note>original: Sa'd al-Bari'</note> and the two stars of Raidan; these are their forms: ٥

### The Fore-spout
<margin>The Fore-spout rises with its directions and forms.</margin>
Then rises **The Fore-spout** <term>al-Fargh al-Muqaddam|The first of two pairs of stars in Pegasus forming the 'spout' of a water bucket</term>, which are two stars of equal magnitude. They are in the second loop of the bucket’s rope; one is northern and the other is southern, separated by the span of a spear. The northern one is accompanied by two faint, nearby stars, separated by more than a hand-span <note>original: fitr</note>. Every "cubit" <note>original: dhira'</note> here refers to a hand-span. The **Barker** is in the north, at the **Belly of the She-Camel** <note>original: Batn al-Naqah</note>. From the direction of Yemen <note>the South</note> is **The Archer** <note>original: al-Rami</note>, which is a bright star. This is its collective form: ٥

Vertical orientation markers "South" and "North" flank the diagram.</image-desc>

### The Rear-spout
Then rises **The Rear-spout** <term>al-Fargh al-Mu'akhkhar|The second pair of stars in Pegasus, completing the square of the Great Bucket</term>. It is like the Fore-spout, except it is slightly wider. To its north is the **Head of the Northern Serpent-bearer** <note>original: Ras al-Hawwa</note>, and to its south is the **Navel of the Fish** <note>original: Surrat al-Hut; corresponding to the star Mirach</note>. From the direction of Yemen rises the **First Frog** <note>original: al-Dafda' al-Muqaddam; the star Fomalhaut</note>, which is the **Tail of the Whale** and the **Severed Hand** <note>original: Kaff Jadhma</note>. This is its collective form: ٥

### The Belly of the Fish
<margin>The Belly of the Fish rises tenth.</margin>
Then rises **The Belly of the Fish** <term>Batn al-Hut|The 28th and final Lunar Mansion, often associated with the constellation Andromeda</term>. It consists of twelve stars resembling a fish; half of them are in the East and half in the West. Its head points to the North and its tail to the South. Its dwelling is in its eastern alignment. The head is the first part to rise. The Belly of the Fish rises with five northern stars from the Rear-spout. The last part of it to rise is the **Little Finger** <note>original: al-Khinsir</note>, which is the first of the stars of **The Triangle** <note>original: al-Muthallathah; the constellation Triangulum</note> to appear. From the Fish, it rises toward the region of the **Seventy** from the **Rear Frog**, along the **Guarded Way**: ٥

<meta>Following the conclusion of the twenty-eight Lunar Mansions on the previous page, the manuscript now shifts its focus to atmospheric and terrestrial phenomena. This page introduces Chapter Ten, which connects the movement of the winds and the occurrence of earthquakes to astrological and seasonal timing.</meta>

# Chapter Ten: On the Blowing of the Winds, Earthquakes, and Lightnings

### Page 78

<image-desc>A circular cosmological diagram representing the relationship between the Earth, the Zodiac, and the winds. At the absolute center is a small circle labeled "The Earth" (al-Ard). Surrounding it is a twelve-pointed geometric star. Inside the points of this star, the four elements are written in red: "Fiery" (Nari), "Earthy" (Ardi), "Airy" (Hawa'i), and "Watery" (Ma'i). The qualities of the zodiac signs—"Cardinal" (Munqalib), "Fixed" (Thabit), and "Mutable" (Mutajassid)—are also noted. In the outer ring, the twelve signs of the Zodiac are written: Aries, Taurus, Gemini, Cancer, Leo, Virgo, Libra, Scorpio, Sagittarius, Capricorn, Aquarius, and Pisces. Radially extending from the circle are the names of various winds associated with these signs, such as the East Wind (al-Saba), the North Wind (al-Shamal), the West Wind (al-Dabur), the South Wind (al-Janub), and others like al-Azyab and al-Nakba.</image-desc>

<margin>
...from the Earth. As for the still winds that have no movement, they are in the air; but the spirit moves in the hollows of the day, and it gathers in some places until it increases as if it were a vent for spirits. We say that lightning in the clouds is like the flash of a sword when it has a strong polish <note>original: saiqlan - referring to the reflective shine of a blade</note> in that place; the sword flashes with its polished surface. When the form looks toward the lightning, sometimes there occurs [an effect]. Earthquakes are caused by winds trapped within the Earth. They may cause springs to break out. Likewise, if earthquakes occur in the sea, they bring forth springs and drown animals and land creatures, as if the sea has exceeded its bounds due to the tremors.
</margin>

<margin>
When the four winds blow from the Earth from eight directions <note>original: asturbat; likely a technical term for regional wind-sectors</note> and then cease. Earthquakes are only from the Earth; when they increase, and the soul—it was mentioned as if it were ancient speech, a metaphor—[they come] from the Earth of the seventy-seven <note>A symbolic number often used in medieval texts to denote a great variety or a specific mystical set</note> as if they were birds. Ayjan al-Hasan <note>Likely a reference to a specific authority or scholar</note> named them twenty-seven as if they were <unclear>birds</unclear>, and among them are those called seventy as if they were birds.
</margin>

The winds are four: the **East Wind** <note>original: al-Saba</note>—which is here called the South—the **West Wind** <note>original: al-Dabur</note>, which is the Oriental wind <note>Note: The text uses traditional, sometimes contradictory, regional naming conventions for wind directions</note>. Those from the east blow toward the east and those from the west toward the west. The winds flowing between the horizons are balanced by the **Side Wind** <term>al-Nakba|A wind that deviates from the four primary cardinal directions</term>.

The sages said: If the wind is eastern on the seventh day [of the month] with humidity, then the flow <note>original: hari; referring to the inundation levels</note> of the Nile will increase, but it will be difficult for the beasts of burden. The mixing of temperaments <note>The four humors of the body</note> and the yield of fruit will increase, as will snakes; thus, it is an ill-omened year. 

If on the eighth day the wind is humid and stormy, the distress of the clouds will settle. Though during the mating season <note>original: al-nazwa</note> the animals may miscarry what is in their wombs, the year's wheat will have full grains. However, illness will persist for a time with much liability. 

If on the ninth day the wind is a gentle humidity, the agitation <note>original: wajba</note> will settle, the year's fertility will increase, dates will be good, and pests will vanish. Though livestock may grow lean, the olives will bear fruit. 

Likewise, the flow of the Nile will be completed. On the tenth day, the same applies to the honey. If the wind is humid on the tenth, the flow [of the Nile] will increase, and if a gentle wind blows in the evening, rain will be plentiful that year. However, storms will be severe for ships, harvests will perish, and the next generation of livestock will be difficult to produce, until things become hard for the people and many of the nobles of the land pass away. 

If the wind is eastern on the eleventh day with humidity, then the year will be fertile, and people will gather together. Growth will increase, the Nile’s flow will be abundant, and the grapes will be of good quality. The flies will die, the children will be safe, and the **Sumjur** <note>original: al-Sumjur; possibly a type of fish or a regional plant</note> will increase. And God knows best.

->**This is the essence of the judgments regarding favorable winds that are not devoid of humidity, and the rulings upon them.**<-

***

<summary>This page discusses meteorology and seismology, explaining earthquakes as trapped winds and providing prognostications based on wind behavior during the first eleven days of a month. It includes a complex diagram mapping the twelve zodiac signs and their elements to specific wind directions.</summary>
<keywords>Winds (Saba, Dabur, Nakba), Earthquakes, Lightning, Nile Inundation, Zodiac, Prognostication, Agriculture, Humors</keywords>

--- Page 44 ---
<meta>This page continues the astrological and meteorological prognostications established in the previous chapter, moving from the general theory of winds and earthquakes to specific predictions based on the calendar. It concludes the first section of the work and transitions into a geographical study based on classical Greek traditions.</meta>

He said: If the sixth day of **Tubah** <note>The fifth month of the Coptic calendar, roughly corresponding to January.</note> falls on a **Sunday**:
Its name in Persian is *Sand*, in Roman <note>referring to Greek: *Kyriake*</note> it is *Karaki*, in Hindi it is *Atwar*, in Coptic it is *Sa’y*, and in Hebrew it is *Ahud* <note>original: *Echad*</note>. In this year, there will be violent windstorms. In the summer, one should fear an abundance of aches and fevers. The plants of the Nile will increase, and there will be fierce fighting. A new kingdom shall appear, the bitterness of the vineyards will lighten, and travel by both sea and land will be safe and prosperous.

If the sixth day of Tubah falls on a **Monday**:
Its name in Persian is *Dastenid*, in Roman it is *Witra* <note>Greek: *Deutera*</note>, in the language of Sindh it is *Surwar*, and in Coptic it is *Sa’o*. In this year, there will be violent windstorms, the crops will be moist, and the Nile will rise high. The Coptic people will suffer from the cold, and there will be many ailments of the liver and other various pains. People will die far from the shelter of trees <note>This likely refers to deaths occurring in the open wilderness or desert.</note>. There will be fighting among the people, the palm trees will be parched, and certain species will become scarce. Sea travel will become difficult, and honey will be in short supply. Camel-rearing <note>original: *bina' al-jamal* - potentially referring to the physical constitution or breeding of camels</note> will be established, flax will be abundant, while gourds and eggplants will decrease. Dew will fall during the hot winds, and the crops will flourish.

If the sixth day of Tubah falls on a **Tuesday**:
Its name in Persian is *Shenid*, in Hindi it is *Akharwa*, in Roman it is *Trithi* <note>Greek: *Trite*</note>, in Coptic it is *Bashman*, and in Hebrew it is *Shalbti* <note>Hebrew: *Shlishi*</note>. There will be winds so violent in their speed that they cause illness, and many ships will be lost at sea. The plague will increase, as will the pressing of vineyards and [the influence of] idols <note>The mention of "idols" here is unusual for this period and may refer to ancient ruins or archaeological finds.</note>. Discord will break out between kings, though there will not be much actual fighting. The Nile will flow well, but many people will fall ill due to the humidity.

If the sixth day of Tubah falls on a **Wednesday**:
Its name in Persian is *Jahar Shenid*, in Hindi it is <unclear>Basa</unclear>, in Roman it is *Tatri* <note>Greek: *Tetarte*</note>, in Coptic it is *Atu*, and in Hebrew it is *Rab’i*. That year will see destructive windstorms, yet sea travel will remain safe. Various species will remain hidden, and fevers will become widespread <unclear>everywhere</unclear>. The practice of sorcery <note>original: *hadd al-sihr* - the "edge" or prevalence of magic</note> will increase, and there will be fatal <unclear>pains</unclear>. The waters of the Nile, seeds, and honey will be abundant. Sheep will perish, corruption will spread among kings, and much will be lost to the dampness.

If the sixth day of Tubah falls on a **Thursday**:
Its name in Persian is *Bakhshanba*, in Hindi it is *Hast*, in Roman it is *Yumna* <note>Greek: *Pempte*</note>, in Coptic it is *Su*, and in Hebrew it is *Hamash*. The winds in that year will be gentle and pleasant. The bitterness of wheat and other grains will decrease. The King will be afflicted by the plague, but sheep will multiply, and honey will be plentiful. There will be a severe valley <note>possibly referring to a flood or a specific geographic event</note>, but sea vessels will be safe. Waters may decrease, yet the land will remain well-irrigated. Those in the service of the Sultan will suffer during the hot winds.

If the sixth day of Tubah falls on a **Friday**:
Its name in Hindi is *Sahruwa*, in Roman it is *Adita*, in Persian it is *Adina*, in Coptic it is *Jad*, and in Hebrew it is *Sab’i*. In that year, there will be gale winds and heavy rain. The grains will grow large, but the fruits of the earth may be ruined. Kings will fight one another. Chickpeas and lentils will be abundant, and the Nile will increase.

If the sixth day of Tubah falls on a **Saturday**:
Its name in Hindi is *Sashwar*, in Persian is *Shenid*, in Roman is *Sabatar* <note>Greek: *Sabbaton*</note>, in Coptic is *Bashba*, and in Hebrew is *Saf’i*. The winds in that year will be gales, the summer will be cold, and the breezes pleasant, but they will harm the sheep. The produce of bees, honey, and flax will increase. The price of food will rise, and corruption will grow among kings. The Nile will be blocked <note>original: *sadd* - meaning the flow is obstructed or the dams are closed</note>, and the year will be difficult for the people of the land during the heat of the sea. There will be many deaths among the sheep. And God knows best.

->**The first treatise is completed with the praise and help of God.**<-

To be followed by the second treatise, which consists of twenty-five chapters. The first chapter concerns the measurement of the Earth and its form, from the Equator to the end of the inhabited world—which is sixty-six degrees—as mentioned by <term>Ptolemy of Pelusium</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Egyptian polymath.</note> in his well-known book, the <term>Almagest</term> <note>The definitive astronomical manual of the Middle Ages, originally titled *Mathematike Syntaxis*.</note> as well.

<summary>This page provides a series of prognostications based on the day of the week the 6th of Tubah falls on, covering weather, agriculture, and politics. It concludes the first treatise and introduces a second section on geography based on Ptolemy’s Almagest.</summary>
<keywords>Tubah, days of the week, multilingual names, Nile flood, agricultural omens, Ptolemy, Almagest, geography, windstorms, political strife</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from astrological and calendar-based predictions into a technical geographical treatise. It links the previous discussions of natural phenomena to the physical dimensions of the Earth, utilizing both classical Greek knowledge and Islamic scientific advancements.</meta>

# The First Chapter of the Second Treatise on the Measurement of the Earth

## And its division into the Seven Climes according to what was mentioned by Ptolemy and others

Ptolemy <note>Claudius Ptolemy (c. 100–170 AD), the Greco-Egyptian mathematician and geographer whose work *Geographia* was foundational to medieval science.</note> said: When I desired to know the greatness of the Earth, its inhabited regions, and its desolate wastes, I derived that from the rising of the sun and its duration. In a day and its night, there are twenty-four parts <note>referring to the 24 hours of a full day</note>. In equal hours, each hour contains fifteen parts <note>degrees of the celestial rotation; $15^\circ \times 24 = 360^\circ$</note>. Then I multiplied the twenty-four hours by the fifteen parts, and the product was three hundred and sixty parts. I then wished to know how many miles a single part <note>one degree</note> would be. I derived this from eclipses of the sun and the moon, observing how many hours the night of a city increases. I divided the miles by the parts of an hour, and it was found that one part equals seventy-five miles. So, I multiplied seventy-five by the three hundred and sixty degrees of the sphere’s circumference, and the sum was twenty-seven thousand miles for the inhabited world.

It was found that from the first of the Inhabited Isles <note>The Canary Islands, often called the Fortunate Isles in antiquity</note> in the Western Sea—which is the Green Sea <note>The Atlantic Ocean</note>—to the furthest reaches of the civilization of China, the distance is twelve hours. Thus, it was known that this is half the circumference of the Earth, which is thirteen thousand five hundred miles of inhabited land. Regarding the Earth's width <note>latitude</note>, he found the extent of the Earth from the southern side to the northern side. The southern line is the Equator, where day and night are equal. The line of the wilderness, which marks the limit of the calculation in the northern direction, is where the redness of the horizon is continuous, and the length of the day there reaches twenty hours, while the night is four hours; then it reverses, and the night becomes twenty hours while the day is four hours. This occurs at a latitude of sixty-six degrees.

The Caliph al-Ma'mun <note>The Abbasid Caliph (reigned 813–833 AD) who established the House of Wisdom in Baghdad and commissioned several scientific expeditions.</note> desired to test the extent of the Earth’s inhabited area, so he dispatched <gloss>astronomers</gloss> to investigate that. They found [measurements at] one hundred and sixty, and eight hundred and seventy, and Barqa. As for <unclear>Badr</unclear>, he saw its inclination from our zenith—which is upon its level surface—to be thirty-four degrees. The city of Raqqa is the same from our zenith as <unclear>Homs</unclear>, and one part emerged as a result. He then measured the distance between the two cities by surveying and physical measurement.

<margin>And when they measured, they knew that the area of</margin>
Every degree of the Greatest Sphere <note>the celestial equator or the Earth's circumference</note> is sixty-six miles and two-thirds of a mile in length. I multiplied those miles by the three hundred and sixty degrees of the Earth’s total circumference, and it was twenty-four thousand miles <gloss>and that is the circumference of the inhabited Earth</gloss>, and its diameter is seven thousand six hundred and eighty miles.

It was found in an ancient section of Ptolemy's reports that the measurement of the Earth is one hundred and eighty thousand *stadia* <note>An ancient Greek unit of distance.</note>. A *stadium* is four hundred cubits, and a mile is three thousand cubits. Thus, we know that a mile is seven and a half *stadia*. If we divide the one hundred and eighty thousand *stadia* by seven and a half to find its equivalent in miles, it becomes clear to us that the circumference of the Earth is twenty-four thousand miles, and its diameter is seven thousand six hundred and six and one-third miles. This applies to the circumference in both length and width.

When al-Ma'mun desired to know the size of the Earth and verify it, he found that Ptolemy had mentioned that its size was such-and-such *stadia*. He asked the translators about the definition of a *stadium*, but they differed in its description, and the master did not add more than what I have added. So he dispatched Khalid ibn Abd al-Malik al-Marwarrudhi, Ali ibn Isa al-Asturlabi <note>“The Astrolabe-maker”</note>, and Ahmad ibn al-Buhturi, the <gloss>Engineer</gloss> and companion, along with a group of skilled surveyors, to craft the instruments they required. He sent them to a location he chose in the Desert of Sinjar. Khalid and a group with him headed toward the North Star <note>original: *Banat Na'sh* - the constellations of the Great or Little Bear used for navigation</note>, just as Ali, Ahmad, and a group with them headed toward the South Pole. Each one of them proceeded until they reached...

<summary>This page describes the medieval transition from Ptolemaic geographical calculations to the empirical measurements commissioned by the Caliph al-Ma'mun. It details the mathematical proofs used to determine the Earth's circumference, settling on the famous figure of 24,000 miles.</summary>
<keywords>Ptolemy, Al-Ma'mun, Sinjar Desert, circumference of the Earth, stadia, miles, latitude, Khalid ibn Abd al-Malik, Ali ibn Isa al-Asturlabi, astronomy, geography</keywords>

--- Page 46 ---
<meta>The previous page introduced the classical Greek measurements of the Earth, specifically Ptolemy’s calculation that one degree of the Earth's circumference equals 75 miles. This page shifts to the scientific corrections made during the Islamic Golden Age under Caliph al-Ma'mun, where scholars conducted empirical field tests to provide more accurate dimensions.</meta>

...the place designated for them. They found the meridian altitude <note>The height of the sun at its highest point at noon.</note> had shifted; it had changed from the meridian altitude they had first measured by exactly one degree, after accounting for the solar declination <note>The angle of the sun relative to the celestial equator, which changes daily.</note> over that distance of travel. They had surveyed the path, marked their progress, and set up markers; then they returned along those same markers and conducted a second measurement to verify. They found the difference between the two points to be one degree of latitude. The distance they had physically surveyed on the surface of the Earth was fifty-six miles and two-thirds of a mile, with an additional four thousand cubits according to the Sudadi Cubit <note>original: "الذراع السودادي" (*al-dhira' al-sawda'*). This is the "Black Cubit," a standard measure established by al-Ma'mun, reportedly based on the arm length of a specific attendant in his court to ensure scientific consistency.</note>, which is the measure Al-Ma'mun established for measuring textiles and for use in this observatory.

Al-Ma'mun also tested the calculated distance between Mecca and Baghdad. Through calculation, he found it to be sixty-nine miles and three and a half and a sixth miles <note>approximately 72 2/3 miles</note> by the Arabian measure. Then he tested this through Khalid <note>Khalid ibn Abd al-Malik al-Marwazi, a prominent 9th-century astronomer.</note> and his companions through physical surveying. They found the distance between Baghdad and Mecca, following the most direct and level path possible, to be seven hundred and twelve miles. Thus, the physical measurement exceeded the theoretical calculation by eight miles and a quarter. 

They then subdivided the Sudadi Cubit: its length was two spans (*shibr*), each span was twelve fingers (*isba'*), each finger was twelve barleycorns (*sha'irah*) laid side-by-side, and each barleycorn was twelve atoms <note>original: "ذرة" (*dharrah*). In this context, it refers to the smallest imaginable unit of measurement, often translated as a speck of dust or an atom.</note>. 

Based on this, the circumference of the Earth is twenty-four thousand miles. This equals ninety-six million cubits, which is one hundred and ninety-eight billion spans, and two billion eight hundred million four thousand fingers, and twenty-seven billion six hundred million forty-eight thousand barleycorns, and two hundred and thirty-one billion seven hundred and seventy-six thousand atoms.

<meta>This page continues the geographic survey from the previous section, moving from a general list of regions to a structured description of the "Seven Climes" (latitudinal zones), a system inherited from Greek geography and expanded by medieval Islamic scholars. It establishes the astrological and physical characteristics of each zone.</meta>

# Chapter Three: On Knowledge of the Climes
-> The Seven [Climes], their conditions, and what lies beyond the Equator toward the North <-

## The First Clime
It is called *Amarash* <note>original: "Amarash" – likely a corruption of a Persian or Greek name for the first latitudinal zone</note> and its beginning is near the Burnt Land <note>The Torrid Zone or the Equator, believed to be too hot for most life</note>. Its Zodiac sign is Sagittarius and its planet is Jupiter. It encompasses the regions of India and Far China. It begins in the East and ends in the West, with a length of one hundred and eighty degrees. Its width extends from the vicinity of the Burnt Land to the region of *Sarandib* <note>modern-day Sri Lanka</note> in the North, spanning eighteen [degrees]. Its inhabitants are from Far China, a people who go naked. At the limit of the seven aspects, their appearance is considered unsightly, their colors are distorted, and their forms are meager. They are bearded and their clothing is distinct. They are a civilized people, possessing knowledge of mathematics and exercises, patient in their intelligence, though their lives are overtaken by the nature of the land of grasses and stones. In their clime, there are animals with massive bodies and bones, and great birds whose bodies are built like those animals. There are elephants, dragons <note>original: "tannin" – refers to large serpents or mythical dragons</note>, wild beasts, and enormous snakes. Some of them have terrifying appearances. There are giraffes and diverse forms, including the likeness of the Phoenix <note>original: "Anqa" – a legendary bird of vast size</note> in their lands. Among them is a world of metamorphosed beings, cattle, and the swarming creatures of the earth. In this clime, there are seventeen mountains, including Mount Sarandib, which is over two hundred and sixty miles long. There is also the mountain surrounding the Sea of *Nafun* <note>Likely the Sea of China or the eastern Indian Ocean</note>, which is shaped like an arc, following this likeness. In this clime, there are twenty-two rivers and springs, including the Spring of *Ari* in India, known as *Mataks*.

<margin>Pearls; its inhabitants are Nearer China, India, and Yemen.</margin>

## The Second Clime
Its name in the Roman language <note>Referring to Greek/Byzantine sources</note> is *Banastananaza*, and Ptolemy called it *Ghyraunos* <note>original: "Ghyraunos" – likely a corruption of "Dia Meroes," the clime passing through Meroë</note>. It is the dwelling of the Abyssinians. Its longest day is thirteen and a half hours. Its Zodiac sign is Aries and its planet is Saturn. Its width extends from the land of Sarandib to the *Khashfa* lands. In it are mines of pure gold <note>original: "al-Uqiyan"</note>, jewels, peridots, and emeralds. It contains powerful stinging insects and jewels whose size is like the *Bahadun* <note>Possibly a local term for a large stone or weight</note> found in the first clime. The appearance of its inhabitants is not as hideous as those mentioned previously. Their land is not without treatments, charms, and knowledge of stones and drugs, except that they have shorter lifespans than the former. They do not have...

<margin>...and twenty-one mountains, including *Karmaz*, which is ninety-eight miles long, for example. In it are...</margin>

...flowing rivers and springs.

<margin>Boundaries</margin>

## The Third Clime
It is named Alexandria, and Ptolemy called it the Clime of Egypt. Its Zodiac sign is Scorpio and its planet is Mars. The beginning of this clime ends at the lower lands of Egypt in the West, and its eastern parts extend from the land of *Nas* to the lands of Isfahan. The Romans <note>Greeks/Byzantines</note> there are handsome, living in an atmosphere of balance and coolness. Its center is Alexandria. Its inhabitants are characterized by their investigation and pursuit of literature and books, their knowledge of words, and their inquiry into matters of religious obedience. They are diligent in seeking manners and sciences and deriving various arts. The noble among its people are known for the excellence of their temperament due to the balance of this clime. In it are thirty-one mountains, and twenty-six large springs and rivers, and one sea-river <note>likely referring to the Nile</note>.

**The Fourth Clime**
Its name in the Roman language is *Rodous* <note>Rhodes</note> and Ptolemy called it *Eirodos* <note>original: "Eirodos" – a corruption of "Dia Rhodos," the clime passing through Rhodes</note>. Its longest day is fourteen hours. Within it are cities, the island [of Rhodes], and Babylon. Its width [extends across several regions], its Zodiac sign is Gemini, and its planet is Mercury. It supports the land of Spain <note>original: "al-Asfan"</note> and encompasses half of the lands of Africa—the northern side of it—as well as Sicily and the lands of the Euphrates, the land of *Abya*, *Troas* <note>Troy</note>, and *Fasin* from the Roman lands and others. It is the center of the Earth and the smallest of the climes in terms of area.

<summary>This page describes the first four of the seven traditional "climes" of the world, detailing their geographical boundaries, astrological associations, and the physical and cultural traits of their inhabitants, from the "Burnt Land" of the south to the temperate center of the world.</summary>

<keywords>Seven Climes, Ptolemy, India, China, Alexandria, Egypt, Abyssinia, Babylon, Astrology, Sarandib, Geography</keywords>

--- Page 49 ---
<meta>This page continues the detailed description of the Seven Climes, specifically concluding the Fourth Clime and describing the Fifth, Sixth, and the beginning of the Seventh. The text focuses on the temperament of the inhabitants, geographical landmarks like mountains and rivers, and astrological associations.</meta>

...And its inhabitants are people of forbearance, philosophy, logic <note>original: "aqd" — literally "binding," referring here to the capacity for complex contracts or systematic thought</note>, engineering, and literature. They are characterized by ambition and an inquiry into existing matters, gemstones, the natures of things, the intellect, the understanding of books, and the wonders of the fine sciences. They possess more knowledge than the people of the Third Clime because of the refinement of their intellect and the balance of their temperaments.

In this clime, there are twenty-four mountains. Among them is the Mountain of Snow <note>Mount Hermon, known in Arabic as Jabal al-Shaykh</note> in Damascus, which is one hundred and eighty miles long; Mount Shayzar, one hundred and forty miles long; Mount al-Lukkam <note>The Amanus Mountains in modern-day Turkey</note>, one hundred miles long; the mountain connected to Halwan, fifteen miles long; a mountain connected to Maydan, forty miles long; and the mountain that overlooks Amid <note>modern Diyarbakır</note>, eight miles long.

In it are twenty-four rivers. Some are so wide they do not flow for more than a distance of one hundred and sixteen miles, such as the Stinking Lake <note>original: "al-Buhayra al-Muntina" — a common medieval name for the Dead Sea due to its sulfurous smell</note>. In it is also the Lake of Tiberias, which is thirty-three miles [in size]. There is a river that leaves Damascus and passes toward al-Katiba, known as the "Vanquished" river. Another river emerges from a mountain, cuts through Istakhr <note>an ancient city in Fars, Iran</note>, and ends in the sea near Siraf; it originates from springs in the East, forms a marsh <note>original: "batiha"</note> of about one hundred and seventy-three miles, then passes by the city of al-Sira and empties into the sea.

# The Fifth Clime
Its name in the Roman tongue is *Qulantara* <note>possibly a corruption of a Greek geographic term</note>, and Ptolemy called it *al-Suntus*. Its [longest] day is fifteen hours. This clime includes Byzantium <note>original: "Lafsantiyya" — likely a corruption of Byzantia</note>, Amorium, and Rome. Its Zodiac sign is Aquarius and its planet is Saturn <note>original: "al-Qinawiyya" — likely a corruption of "al-Kaywaniyya," from the Persian name for Saturn, Kaywan</note>. It includes Al-Andalus <note>Muslim Iberia</note>. Its inhabitants are people of proud bearing <note>original: "sere jerd" — from the Persian "sar-gard," meaning high-headed or stout-hearted</note>, though they can be prone to folly, recklessness, harshness, and a nomadic wildness, despite their strength. They possess manners, and they are quick to accept education and understand it through their books. They possess intellect, though their minds are weaker than those of the people of the Fourth Clime.

In it are twenty-nine mountains. Among them are Harith and Huwayrith <note>The traditional Arabic names for Great and Little Ararat</note>, which are one hundred and eighty miles long. There is the mountain between Mosul and Shahrizor, one hundred and forty-five miles long, and the mountain connected to the lands of the Khazars and the region of the "Gate of Gates" <note>original: "Bab al-Abwab," referring to Derbent</note>, which is one hundred and seven miles long. There is also the mountain of Tabaristan which overlooks Sabur and Jurjan, four hundred and forty miles long. In it are fifteen rivers, including the Tigris River, which spans five hundred miles from its minor source; it flows through Kajard and passes through Jiri at the base of the mountains of Harith and Huwayrith for a distance of one hundred miles, extending seventy miles. In it also is the Oxus <note>original: "Jayhun"</note>, which measures three hundred miles.

# The Sixth Clime
In the Roman tongue, it is called *Masu-yutomist*. Its Zodiac sign is Virgo and its planet is Mars. The inhabitants of this clime are the Bulgars <note>original: "al-Burjan"</note> and the Slavs <note>original: "al-Saqaliba"</note>. In one region of this clime, there is a city inhabited by women without men. These are the ones known in the Roman tongue as the *Murtha* <note>A corruption of "Amazons"</note>. They are known now for [cauterizing] the [right] breast of girls while they are young, hating it because it might hinder them from fighting, wars, and combat. They call them the "Divided Ones" <note>original: "akhruban ba'in"</note> because they are distinguished by their fighting. They prefer their children to be male... <insert>text unclear</insert>... It is said that every year they descend toward the people of the land of Hormuza and the land of the Bulgars, where they consort with the men of the Bulgars, become pregnant, and then return to their own places, prepared for war. This is a famous matter that no scholar denies.

The inhabitants of this clime are lovers of war, shedders of blood, and given to humiliation, deceit, and oppression. Because of this, they were named the Slavs <note>The author likely implies a false etymology here</note>. They have no knowledge of literature, especially the sciences. In it are twenty-four mountains, including Mount al-Zahaq... <unclear>fana mankalan</unclear>... which is connected to the sea and is one hundred and four miles long. In it are twenty-six rivers, including the Euphrates, which measures seven hundred and thirty miles. Among them is the river that passes between the Khazars and Hurzan, then the lands of Armenia... <unclear>wa-basanda rab bardha'a</unclear>... its distance is seven hundred miles. It is known for its moisture and flows from West to East. In this clime, there are springs that do not flow.

# The Seventh Clime
In the Roman tongue, it is called *Parsiyash*, and Ptolemy named it *Barushnash*. Its Zodiac sign is Libra and from...

<summary>This page describes the human and physical geography of the Fourth, Fifth, and Sixth Climes, including a legendary account of the Amazons (Murtha) living among the Slavs and Bulgars.</summary>
<keywords>Seven Climes, Ptolemy, Damascus, Dead Sea, Ararat, Al-Andalus, Slavs, Bulgars, Amazons, Tigris, Euphrates</keywords>

--- Page 50 ---
<meta>This page concludes the description of the northernmost regions and their inhabitants before transitioning to a detailed geographical breakdown of the Arabian Peninsula. It continues the previous page's catalog of mountains and rivers, moving from the habitable climes into the more legendary or extreme northern and southern reaches, including the mythical lands of Gog and Magog and the legendary "Islands of Women and Men."</meta>

The Samar and... the people who are called Turmidh and Nu <note>original: "Turmidh wa Nu"—likely referencing remote northern or Central Asian nomadic tribes, possibly a corruption of Turkic or Scythian names</note>, and among them are a people of small stature and creation. They do not reside in [known] settled lands due to their distance to the north and their presence in unknown and uninhabited places. Nomadic Arabs live at their same latitude; their orbits, their livestock, and their cattle revolve around them, and they are very small. No others can live in these lands due to the severity of the cold. Nothing can be harvested in their lands during their seasons.

They are unable to build houses with brick and stone; rather, their houses are made of woven grass which they carry on carts pulled by these cattle and from... wherever they find grazing for their animals, they settle. They are the wretched of their time, living in the worst of conditions. When they are afflicted with difficult illnesses, they place their sick upon the carts; the males face the sun and the females face the north <note>A ritual or folk-medical practice based on the direction of the winds or celestial bodies</note>, and in this way, they seek recovery.

In this clime, there are twenty-four mountains. Among them is the Mountain of Gog and Magog <note>original: "Yajuj wa Majuj"—legendary figures in Islamic and Judeo-Christian eschatology, often placed behind a great barrier in the far north</note>, which is called "The Encompassing." Its length is nine hundred and twenty-eight miles. Through it passes the Jaxartes <note>original: "Sayhun"</note> river, the length of which is four hundred and seventy-six miles; from its beginning to its end is one thousand and two hundred miles. It emerges from a pass, enters the sea, and then rises like the sea. Its distance is four hundred and nineteen miles. The "Mother of Bones" river <note>original: "Umm al-’Izam"</note> has a length of one thousand six hundred and thirty miles, and its mouth is in a land beyond... one of... and a river that pours into the land of Gog and Magog. He describes these rivers in their distance across the continent filled with... and what was outward at the Equator toward the south is a healthy state of comparable breadth, the longest being from four hundred to five hundred [miles]. There is another mountain, nine hundred miles long, and the Mountain of Al-Muhimm, one thousand miles long; part of it is in the First Clime and the rest is behind the Equator, where the source of the Nile River and its branches are found.

As for what is in Tihama, the land of Sharu... it results from the latitude... which is called a Clime. When the day is long in the northern direction, the inhabited portion increases until it reaches twenty-one hours and twenty-two minutes of an hour, and until it ends at twenty-four hours <note>A description of the "Midnight Sun" phenomenon in the Arctic regions</note>. The light of the day continues as such until... then the night there becomes six months and the day six months.

As for the islands among the rivers, especially in the Green Sea <note>original: "al-Bahr al-Akhdar"—often referring to the Indian Ocean or the surrounding World Ocean in medieval geography</note>, there are one hundred large rivers in its islands. All the rivers in the islands total seventy rivers. Among them, in the Island of Tayy, there are twelve rivers; in the Island of Luba, fourteen rivers; and in the Island of Safdna, five rivers, and its marshy plain is about one hundred and thirty miles. In the Island of Amstafuh—which is attributed to women because [there are only] rivers on this island... a group [of scholars] claimed that there are no creatures there except women, and that they conceive every year from a wind that blows at a known time, and they give birth to nothing but girls. In the Island of Amwabush—which is also attributed to men—there is no woman who... or a marsh for all the rivers of the earth connected to the land... ninety-eight. 5

### Chapter Four: On the Naming of the Arabian Peninsula 5

This is the region between Al-Qadisiyyah and Hadramawt. The Arabs name its regions Tihama, Al-Hijaz, Al-Najd, and Al-Arud. As for **Tihama**, it is where the valleys flow toward the sea. The mountains located in Yemen and the Hijaz are what form the barrier between the Tihama and the Arud. As for what lies between Yemen and the Najd, it is called **Hijaz** <note>The word "Hijaz" literally means "the barrier" or "the partition"</note> because it partitions the Najd from the Arud. **Al-Najd** is the plateau of the land, the highlands and plains that lead toward the East. **Al-Arud** is the side from Aden to Al-Yamama. The area defined in the lowlands is called Hijaz because it is a stone [barrier] between the lowland—which is descending—and the Najd—which is the highland behind it. The mountain in Asia to the south, the Black Peak, its height was rugged... and the plains of its land are the circular regions.

<meta>catchword: which</meta>

<summary>This page describes the extreme northern peoples living in grass huts on carts and the mythical islands of the "Green Sea," including the Island of Women. It then transitions into a formal geographic definition of the Arabian Peninsula, dividing it into Tihama, Hijaz, Najd, and Al-Arud based on topography.</summary>
