<meta>This page continues from the previous geographical survey, transitioning from the description of northern mountains and legendary tribes to the etymology of major regions (Yemen, the Levant, and Iraq) and a medical-geographical analysis of how climate affects human health and character.</meta>

...their vulnerabilities are combined for all that. What lies to the east of the mountain in terms of deserts extends to the borders of Iraq, the Sindh <note>The region around the Indus River in modern-day Pakistan</note>, and what follows them. All of this is bounded. We call Yemen "Yemen" because it is to the right <note>original: "yamin"—the right side</note> of the Kaaba, and the Levant is called "Sham" because it is to the left <note>original: "sh'am"—the left side</note> of the Kaaba. Iraq is called "Iraq" because of the proximity of the waters of the Tigris, the Euphrates, and the other rivers; this name is derived from the *‘ara* <note>original: "‘ara"—the leather thong or handle of a bucket, implying the region is at the "water's edge"</note> of a bucket.

Others said that when the tongues were divided at Babel <note>Babylon</note>, people distinguished themselves; some took the "right of the sun," which is the right side for the Levant <note>In traditional Arabic orientation, one faces East; thus North (the Levant) is to the left and South (Yemen) is to the right</note>, and they were marked by this name. Others said the Levant was named after Sham <note>Shem</note> son of Noah—peace be upon him—because he was the first to settle and inhabit it, making the Arabs its residents, derived from the word *sha’ama*. Others said it was named the Levant [Sham] because the land contains red, black, and white patches, and its color is like *shām* <note>original: "shām"—beauty marks or moles, referring to the variegated colors of the landscape</note>. Yemen was named after Yemen son of Qahtan son of Abir, as the Arabs "went to the right" <note>original: "tayammanat"—to turn right or seek a blessing</note> to settle there.

### Chapter Five: Regarding the Cities of the Horizons

God Almighty divided the earth into sections, placing some above others in elevation or depth, and created inhabitants and dwellings suited to their locations and paths. Every land with a balanced climate and light water produced people whose appearance and character suited their proximity and territory in their way of life. Every land that deviated from balance caused its people to fall into poor conditions. 

As for the southern cities in Africa, the water there is plentiful but salty; it heats up in summer and cools in winter. The temperaments of the inhabitants are phlegmatic <note>original: "balghamiya"—one of the four Galenic humors, associated here with sluggishness and moisture</note> and their constitutions are corrupted. Weakness, laxity, incapacity, fear, feebleness, and failure dominate their bodies. Their women are sickly due to excessive menstruation and a lack of healthy children because of excessive moisture. The children are prone to ailments such as asthma, tetanus <note>original: "kuzar"—spasms or lockjaw</note>, hidden sicknesses, and inflammatory heat; indeed, they die from exhausting fevers and debilitating pleurisy <note>original: "dhat al-janb"—an inflammation of the chest lining</note>.

As for the cities in the northern direction and its furthest reaches, where cold winds prevail, their waters are dry <note>likely referring to ice or hard water</note> and slow to digest. The people have thick necks and are well-built. They are strong and mighty, with slender, light legs but broad chests. They are prone to pustules on their extremities, and their bellies are firm. Their heads are hard and dry due to the lack of excess waste in their bodies. They do not suffer from ophthalmia <note>original: "ramad"—an inflammatory eye disease</note>, but if they do contract it, their eyes split open from the severity of the cold. Their lives are often long, and their illnesses are acute. Their women are barren due to the coldness of the wombs <note>original: "manasim"—literally the passages or vents of the body</note> and slow recovery; if they do conceive, they give birth with great difficulty. Children are prone to "yellow fullness" <note>original: "al-mala al-asfar"—likely referring to neonatal jaundice or fluid discharge</note> in the ears and its phantoms.

As for the cities where the sun rises, their dwellings are situated between cold and hot winds. Their waters are bright, refreshing, clear, and moist. The trees are thick, and the appearance of their people is handsome and balanced; the temperament of their land has few diseases. 

As for the cities in the path of the West, where the eastern winds and the blowing of hot and cold winds meet, this region is poor and prone to many diseases and symptoms because their waters are not sweet and their trees are very tall due to the thickness of the air. Its people are sallow and sickly; their temperament and air lack a pleasant balance after the sun rises over them, and their voices are heavy. 

Hippocrates <note>original: "Biqrat"—the famous Greek physician whose works were central to medieval Arabic medicine</note> mentioned in the *Book of Airs and Places* <note>original: "Kitab al-Ahwiya wa-l-Buldan"—referring to the Hippocratic treatise "Airs, Waters, and Places"</note> that the primary causes are the extremes, particularly in the North, where northern winds are constant and southern winds are calm. This is why the southern regions... from the mountains... there is no lack of snow at all times. In most of the lands of Armenia, there are cities where the nature of the people is not separate from the nature of the snow for six months; that is when the sun becomes...

<meta>This page continues the geographic and ethnographic survey. Having previously established the etymology of Yemen, the Levant, and Iraq, the author now transitions into "medical geography"—the belief that climate and the positions of the stars directly dictate the physical appearance, health, and moral character of different peoples. This section concludes the analysis of northern and southern extremes before beginning the famous entry on the legendary Land of Waq-Waq.</meta>

...in the southern zodiac signs <term>al-buruj al-janubiyya</term> <note>These are the signs of the zodiac positioned south of the celestial equator</note>. Even if animals are numerous there, they perish in that year by a widespread mortality; they are forced into their dens for many long months. Likewise, there is a continuous arrival of heavy snows; the moisture of the snow overwhelms their bodies, causing physical lethargy and aching joints due to excessive anxiety and the wasting away of the face. Their grief increases because of the coldness of their air, for a cold temperament <note>In medieval medicine, the "temperament" or *mizaj* was the balance of heat, cold, moisture, and dryness in the body</note> generates harshness in their bodies and their flesh. Their skin colors are livid <note>original: "khudr"—literally green, but used here to describe a pale, ashen, or dark-leaden complexion caused by cold</note>. Among them is foolishness and ignorance [affecting] their fingers and feet. The character of people in these regions is marked by harshness, a lack of justice and knowledge, the severing of family ties, and a dislike for ascetics.

As for the scorched, feverish places where, due to the intensity of the heat, neither animal nor plant finds moisture—because of the extreme heat and its poisonous nature—a condition develops there. For example, the Sudan and the Abyssinians <note>historical terms used to describe the peoples of Sub-Saharan and East Africa</note> are the inhabitants of the lands between the Tropic of Aries <note>The Celestial Equator</note> and the Tropic of Cancer. The sun’s ascent and descent, when it is in those zodiac signs, is in the middle of the <unclear>sky</unclear> directly over their heads. This heats their air and their environment, so heat and dryness increase within them. Consequently, their colors became black, their hair curly, and their bodies light, while their temperaments are hot. The same applies to their beasts and trees.

# Discourse on the Land of Waq-Waq and its Wonders

*   **Southern Zodiac Signs:** The signs of the zodiac associated with the southern hemisphere.
*   **The Sudan:** Historically referring to the "Land of the Blacks" in Sub-Saharan Africa.
*   **The Abyssinians:** People of the region of modern-day Ethiopia and Eritrea.
*   **Tropic of Aries:** The celestial equator, where the sun sits during the equinox.
*   **Tropic of Cancer:** The northernmost point where the sun can be directly overhead.
*   **Land of Waq-Waq:** A legendary island or region in medieval Arabic geography, often placed in the Indian Ocean or near East Asia.
*   **Wonders:** Marvels or miraculous phenomena (*'aja'ib*).
*   **Waq-Waq Tree:** A mythical tree said to bear fruit in the shape of human heads or animals that cry out "Waq Waq!" when they ripen.

# Discourse on the Waq-Waq Tree

<note>The Waq-Waq tree is a staple of medieval Islamic legend, described by geographers and travelers as growing on a remote island in the far reaches of the Indian Ocean or near China. The "fruit" of the tree were said to be living beings—often beautiful women or animals—who were attached to the branches by their hair. When they ripened and fell, they would emit a cry that sounded like "Waq Waq," which gave the island its name.</note>

->**[Illustration of the Mythical Waq-Waq Tree]**<-

The illustration depicts the legendary Waq-Waq tree with a thick, sturdy central trunk decorated with vertical stripes of red and green. Its wide branches are heavy with green leaves and bear a strange fruit: human figures. These figures, painted in a reddish-brown earth tone, are shown in various states of growth; some hang precariously from the branches by their hair or feet, while others appear to be sitting or standing among the foliage. 

Each figure is crowned with a circular yellow halo or cap <note>In this context, the halo likely signifies the vitality or the "ripening" of the living fruit rather than holiness</note>. At the very base of the tree, situated on a dark, rounded mound of earth, several human figures lie prostrate on the ground, representing the fruit that has fully ripened and fallen from the branches. The entire scene is enclosed in a simple gold-colored frame. Above the tree, a horizontal panel features a line of large, flowing Arabic calligraphy <term>khatt</term> <note>The art of beautiful writing, highly esteemed in Islamic manuscript tradition</note> inscribed in red ink, identifying the subject.

# Chapter Six: On the Shapes of the Seas
-> and their islands and distances <-

It is not within the power of any created being to fully encompass the handiwork of the Lord of the Worlds. Those who take it upon themselves to seek knowledge do so only through observation or what they have heard with their own ears from many sources. We have recorded here what we have heard or observed and what we found of interest, and we have exerted ourselves in explaining the experiences of the wise regarding the seas. These accounts are gathered from every age concerning the sight of the sea. I have presented how the drawing of nautical charts has come to be, according to their actual shapes. In truth, when the seas are stirred by storms, agitation, and fierce winds, it becomes difficult to maintain the accuracy of their drawings upon a flat surface.

Based on the paths of those who travel them, some seas flow over the face of the earth for many miles and leagues. The people of the Indian seas call these <term>khors</term> <note>original: *akhwar*; an Arabic term for an inlet, creek, or estuary where the sea reaches into the land</note>, and the people of the Arabic seas likewise call them *khawr*. Each of these possesses great length and varying widths, as we have explained in the section regarding their origins. Sometimes, a great multitude <note>original: *jund*, literally meaning 'army' or 'host', here referring to a vast body of water or perhaps a settlement</note> might reside within that *khawr*, or it might surround a great city, providing it protection. At other times, the smaller parts of it might be submerged.

The maritime routes have been overtaken by the sea. Abu al-Hasan <term>al-Mas'udi</term> <note>A famous 10th-century Arab historian and geographer known as the "Herodotus of the Arabs."</note> (may God have mercy on him) mentioned in his books many reports concerning the wonders of the land and the sea. As for the land, he claimed that part of the land of <note>Kufa: an important historical city in Iraq</note> Kufa was once a deep sea. Likewise, in the land of Alexandria, the place where the descendants of Hud and their followers lived was once a crushing sea. Conversely, there were seas in ancient times that were traversed as dry land, but the sea rose over them in a single night, rushing in from the direction of the Equator and turning the land into sea. The low-lying land was submerged, while the elevated land remained like high peaks. 

Regarding the true shapes of the seas as seen by man in their length and width, we shall present—that is, we shall strive to follow—what <term>Ptolemy</term> <note>Claudius Ptolemy, the 2nd-century Greco-Roman mathematician and geographer whose work was foundational to Islamic cartography.</note> mentioned in his famous book <term>Geography</term> <note>original: *Jughrafiya*; referring to Ptolemy's *Geographia*.</note>. He described them as an arrangement of curves, angles, squares, and hollows. These shapes are real, but when they are depicted with precision and instruments, it is impossible to complete them perfectly in all their circuits or angles due to the narrowness of the space, whereas on the flat earth, they possess vast expanses. Because of this difficulty, we have created these drawings in this form so that everyone may recognize their own land.

<summary>This page introduces the sixth chapter on maritime geography, discussing the difficulty of mapping the seas and citing historical geographers like al-Mas'udi and Ptolemy. It explains how land and sea have shifted over time, with former land becoming submerged and vice versa.</summary>
<keywords>maritime geography, khors, inlets, al-Mas'udi, Ptolemy, Alexandria, Kufa, cartography, Geographia, environmental change</keywords>

> This is the description of these seas, which are in reality a single sea. Some call it the Great Sea of the Earth.
> The length of this sea from the west to the east is six thousand one hundred and fifty-two parasangs <note>A *farsakh* or parasang is roughly 3-3.5 miles</note>, and its width is twenty parasangs at its narrowest point.

> This sea—which some call the Sea of the Romans, the Greeks, or Latakia—is the one that emerges from the Encompassing Ocean <note>The Atlantic</note> and enters the Levantine Sea at Gibraltar <note>original: *Jabal Tariq*</note>. Near Constantinople, it is called the Jordanian, and the empire of Darius son of Darius <note>Darius III, the last Achaemenid King</note> reached this point, until it is said to be between Constantinople and the [lands of] the Georgeans <note>original: *Jurjis*</note> meeting the Levantine Sea.

This sea has no connection to any of the other seas on the face of the earth, except for what flows into it from the river of the Rus, known as the Atil <note>The Volga River.</note>. If a man were to travel around this sea, he would return to the place where he started; no barrier would prevent him, nor would anything sever his path. It is a stagnant sea with no tide, neither rising nor ebbing. It is dark and foul-smelling; nothing rises from it except fish. Ships sail upon it from the lands of the Muslims to the land of the Khazars, and it is shallow-bottomed.

### Chapter Twelve: In description of the Island of Sicily and mention of the islands attached to it, as the objective in this is brevity

The Island of Sicily is the most significant of the Islamic islands in standing and the most renowned in mention, due to the concentration of its western regions with rural life and the enduring condition of its people, their manners, and their children throughout the ages. 
<note>Sicily was under Islamic rule from the 9th to the 11th century, becoming a major center of culture and trade.</note>
It is noted that vanity, stubbornness, and litigation are prevalent among them. The city itself is called **Palermo** <note>original: "Balarm"</note>, and it is surrounded by a great, towering wall. Its structure is densely packed, appearing almost as if it were a single solid block.
<note>The OCR "مأكولة وجبة حطها القصار" is likely a corrupted description of the city's ancient masonry or its tiered layout.</note>

It was gathered in time into a city known as *al-Khulasa* <note>meaning "The Essence" or "The Elite district"; likely referring to the fortified administrative center known as al-Khalisa</note>, which also has a wall and four gates. Nearby is the district of Qasr Sa’d, and other locations like al-Safa. 
The city's walls enclose it, and most of the markets are located by the Mosque of the Slavs <note>original: "Saqlab"</note> and the New District. All the markets are outside the wall except for the cloth merchants and a group of craftsmen, scholars, and elite citizens. 

Then there is the district of the **Sakaliba** <note>The Slavs; refers to the Saqaliba, or European mercenaries and formerly enslaved people who formed a distinct social class in the Islamic Mediterranean.</note> by the wall of Mundar. In the city, there are one hundred and fifty mosques <note>OCR: "بجو مالة وخمسين" likely "مائة وخمسين"</note> with permanent staff and residents. It features well-known springs such as **Al-Mador**, Fartaja, al-Fula, al-Saf’a, al-Bayda, al-Uryan, and others. A multitude of scholars are found there; it is mentioned in a book found in Andalusia that their number is vast due to their diligence in learning, though others claim they are characterized by conceit. 

The character of its people is generally secretive, and they tend to dislike strangers. Some among them even incline toward the Byzantines <note>original: "al-Rum"</note> in rebellion if the opportunity arises, out of fear. Their language is a mixture of their origins <note>A reference to Siculo-Arabic, a dialect influenced by Greek and Romance languages.</note>, and the revenue of its governors and residents is estimated at twenty thousand dinars. It is a center for medicine, administration, and engineering. To the north, twenty thousand dinars are directed [in trade or tax], as mentioned by Al-Muhallabi in his register. 
<note>Abu al-Husayn al-Muhallabi was a 10th-century geographer known for his "Book of Routes and Realms."</note>
However, there is a perceived lack of wisdom among the common folk and a corruption of intellect, though their leaders are satisfied with them. 

The city’s layout is elongated and full of markets. Regarding the "Palace" <note>original: "al-Qasr"; referring to the Cassaro, the historic central district</note>, it is the seat of the city’s power. Its gates include:
*   The **Sea** Gate <note>original: "Bab al-Bahr"</note>, which looks out toward the water.
*   The gate known as the **Gate of al-Hasan ibn Abi Shu’ayb**, which is an ancient gate.
*   A gate connecting to the **New** City <note>original: "al-Hadith"</note>, frequented by the righteous and spiritual people.
*   The **Gate of the Markets** <note>original: "Bab al-Aswaq"</note>, which is the oldest of its gates.
*   The **Gate of the Sudan** <note>original: "Bab al-Sudan"; likely referring to Black African guards or residents</note>, facing the blacksmiths.
*   The Gate of the New City, through which one exits to the **Jewish** Quarter <note>original: "Jarat al-Yahud"</note>. 

In total, the city has nine gates. The city is long and its markets are bustling. It was said that the Magians <note>original: "Majus"; in this context, it often referred to Viking raiders or non-Christian/non-Muslim foreigners</note> used to plant in the outskirts known as the "Iron Field," where there was an iron mine belonging to the **Aghlabids** <note>The Aghlabid dynasty (800–909) who conquered Sicily for the Abbasid Caliphate.</note>. 

It is remarked that their minds are dull because of their excessive consumption of onions and their little knowledge of broad beans. 
<note>This is a famous humorous trope found in medieval Arabic geography, specifically Ibn Hawqal, who blamed the "corruption" of the Sicilian intellect on their raw onion diet.</note>
The people are difficult for a Sultan to rule, similar to the markets of Samarkand, Ardabil, Mecca, Damascus, Sana’a, and Egypt; a Sultan cannot reconcile with them unless he redresses their grievances. 

Historically, Sicily was under the authority of the Aghlabids. After the Byzantines, it was raided by a Berber commander named Malfun <unclear>possibly Manyakis</unclear>. During the days of the Caliph **Al-Mutawakkil**, a great conquest occurred where a fortified castle was taken after a long struggle. He intended to reside there and built a mosque. When **Ibrahim al-Aghlabi** campaigned, he faced great difficulties until many of his kin perished, but eventually, he penetrated the heart of Sicily and conquered most of it. 

The first raids occurred during the days of **Muawiya ibn Abi Sufyan** <note>The first Umayyad Caliph, reigned 661–680 AD.</note>, when an expedition was sent in the year [...] from the city of al-Qali. In it were found treasures from the days of the Caliph al-Mutawakkil, including the Fortress of Multana and the lands of lower Sicily, where there were idols of gold and silver encrusted with jewels. These were sent to Muawiya. I also saw the likeness of lions and leopards there, much like I saw in the city of Samarkand.

<summary>This page describes the geography, social customs, and history of Palermo and Islamic Sicily, detailing its fortified gates, scholarly reputation, and its conquest by the Aghlabids and Umayyads.</summary>
<keywords>Sicily, Palermo, al-Khalisa, Aghlabids, Medieval Geography, Caliph al-Mutawakkil, Muawiya ibn Abi Sufyan, Slavs, Jewish Quarter, Mediterranean history</keywords>

<meta>The previous page provided a geographical index of the medieval world, including regions from the Levant to the lands of the Slavs and the Rus. ### Chapter Twelve: Concerning the Founding of Mahdiya ###

When the Caliphate passed to the Imam Abu Abd Allah Muhammad al-Mahdi Billah, Prince of the Believers—may God sanctify his soul—he settled at Raqqada <note>A palatial city near Kairouan in modern-day Tunisia, formerly the seat of the Aghlabid dynasty</note>. He was a man of sound judgment and decisive action. The Banu al-Aghlab <note>The Aghlabid dynasty, whom the Fatimids overthrew</note> and their men had fled from it. He observed the palaces of al-Masila and the dwellings of his subjects, and he found Raqqada at that time to be without the protection of a wall, a trench, or a secure entrance.

It was as if the <term>beginning of the Chapter of the Gathering</term> <note>original: "awwal surat al-hashr"; a reference to the Quranic chapter discussing the exile and gathering of people</note> was upon him. He was walking one day through al-Hamra <note>The "Red Palace" area</note>, and he saw that the site was ready for struggle, with no easy exit from one side to the other. He declared to his creation: "I have built in a place not previously chosen." He selected the site and built Mahdiya upon the rock alone as the seat of his kingdom, and its importance grew. He perfected the construction therein.

In the digging of his city, Mahdiya, the salty sea surrounded it from all sides except for the western side. He fortified it with a wall and iron gates of masterful craftsmanship. The beginning of the work was on Saturday, five days having passed of the month of Dhu al-Qa'da in the year 303 <note>equivalent to May 916 AD</note>. He extended the <term>Lion's Spear</term> <note>original: "rumh al-asad"; likely a ritual measurement of the city's boundaries</note>, and when he placed what he had dug for its secret foundations, he ordered some of the servants to calculate a hundred to be raised by arrows, and the lot fell. Either it is the fortified city which is <unclear>beside the clear path</unclear>, they prayed.

Abu Yazid <note>A Kharijite rebel leader known as "The Man on the Donkey," the primary antagonist of the early Fatimids</note> was permitted by this, then he said to his companions: "I only inhabit this time." Then it was the afternoon of Abu Yazid until the end of the day. Then the Caliph built his palaces in the city and resolved to move there. He compelled the people of the state to leave what they had settled in Raqqada—the palaces and the <unclear>lofty</unclear> houses—and to seek its air. So they sold their stones and labor. He said to them: "Those who are currently reluctant will soon hasten toward it." The matter did not pass until the rains ceased, the year became difficult, and we grew old from that. Dwellings were ruined, roofs fell, and famine came. The rebellious fled in strength to the distant lands.

They all refused, but then they leaned toward al-Mahdi and the source of provisions from the support of Mahdiya. He settled them. Then Abu Yazid—may God curse him—emerged with many of the Berbers. In his time in the seat of the Sultanate, he was called by one named Hawaya, and her name was Sankuk. It is said in his biography that he ruled the <unclear>dust-covered ones</unclear> and that he brought the sword, the hardship, and the captivity to those of noble nature. They passed through the <unclear>secret</unclear>, terrifying the cities, ruining the homes, and enslaving the free. He set the sword upon the young and the old. His ministers included one whose language was called Abu Ammar, who was blind, and another called Khammar, who was crippled, and another called Abu Mansur.

Al-Mahdi—peace be upon him—passed away in those days, fourteen nights into the month of Rabi’ al-Akhir in the year 322 <note>equivalent to April 934 AD</note>. His Caliphate lasted twenty-five years, three months, and seven days. Mahdiya had been established on Sunday, nine days into Dhu al-Hijja in the year 308 <note>equivalent to May 921 AD</note>. It was a praiseworthy year. The matter then passed to <term>al-Qa'im bi-Amr Allah</term> <note>The second Fatimid Caliph</note>. Abu Yazid went out against him, gathering crowds and ruining the lands he reached, wandering until <unclear>he rode</unclear> the heights. He would leave those places wearing a white cap and prostrate while riding a <term>gray donkey</term> <note>original: "himar ashhab"; this is why he was nicknamed "Sahib al-Himar"</note>.

In this state of heroes and women, even one from <unclear>al-Mu'izz</unclear> <note>possibly referring to the future Caliph al-Mu'izz</note>... at Mecca from five hundred thousand riders, and they added from the Berbers and from those after them a hundred thousand... his army contained a hundred <unclear>thousand</unclear>... and he marched to Mahdiya and besieged it... and the people suffered from distress in Mahdiya. They went out in groups, and he ordered that whoever fled should be executed. Some of the Berbers lost their way... thirty-five dinars... and they were stabbed in their bellies... and it was said of their seduction that they did not find a hundred thousand dinars. When he marched to the city, the archer extended the digging of a trench around its lands. He—may God curse him—stayed there, standing firm in his condition, preparing to cross the newly made trench. He prayed at the prayer place on Mount <unclear>al-Fath</unclear>, which was built by Ibrahim bin Abd al-Rahman... <unclear>may God's curse be upon him</unclear>... the King saw her... and the fortified building of the Man of the Donkey—<unclear>may God curse him</unclear>—in a place...

**Vocabulary:**
**Al-Mahdi Billah:** The first Fatimid Caliph and founder of the dynasty.
**Mahdiya:** The coastal capital city established by al-Mahdi in modern Tunisia.
**Abu Yazid:** A Kharijite rebel who led a massive revolt against the Fatimids.
**Raqqada:** The former Aghlabid capital city near Kairouan.
**The Berbers:** The indigenous ethnic groups of North Africa.
**Al-Qa'im bi-Amr Allah:** The second Fatimid Caliph, son of al-Mahdi.
**Year 303 AH:** The beginning of the construction of Mahdiya.

<summary>This page recounts the founding of the city of Mahdiya by the first Fatimid Caliph, al-Mahdi Billah, and the subsequent violent rebellion led by the Kharijite Abu Yazid, known as the "Man on the Donkey." It details the strategic shift of the capital from Raqqada to the coastal stronghold of Mahdiya and the immense hardships, including famine and siege, faced by the Fatimid state during its infancy.</summary>

<keywords>Fatimid Caliphate, Mahdiya, al-Mahdi Billah, Abu Yazid, Kharijite Rebellion, Raqqada, North African History, Al-Qa'im bi-Amr Allah, Islamic Architecture, Siege warfare</keywords>

This page transitions from that narrative history into a practical maritime itinerary and a symbolic diagram of the region's coastal defenses and geography.</meta>

From the pillar of proof and evidence, they turned away and persisted in their distancing with arguments. He has clarified his sect, and the dust that cleared from the darkness has been revealed. By them, against the vanguard of the rabble and the ignorant <note>original: "al-ghaugha wa al-hamaj"; terms used historically to describe disorganized mobs or the unrefined masses</note>, do not rejoice in the narrowness of a path that seems to open, for the constriction of guidance belongs to those who ascend the ranks. How often has a sea passed in the darkness of the wayward, wherein a foot slipped amidst the display [of vanity]. Such was the state of his mother—what descended of his command, his wakefulness, and his hardship at the hand of the one who is consistent with God.
١٣٢١ <note>1321 AH, which corresponds to approximately 1903–1904 AD; this date likely marks the transcription of this specific manuscript copy</note>

<meta>This page continues the geographical and statistical description of key cities within the Fatimid sphere of influence, moving from the coastal itineraries of North Africa to a detailed account of Akhmim in Upper Egypt. The handwriting is consistent with the previous sections, focusing on urban infrastructure and economic capacity.</meta>

### **Chapter Fourteen: On the Reports of Egypt** <note>The heading says "Khirab" which usually means ruins, but the context "Khabar" meaning reports or news fits the descriptive nature of the text better.</note> Muhammad bin Abd al-Salam al-Hasani mentioned in his book, composed to describe the beauties of Akhmim <note>An ancient city in Upper Egypt, famous since antiquity for its temples and textile industry</note>, the fourth [climate], regarding the healthiness of its air and the goodness of its people's temperaments and their crafts. He noted that the dead there do not have their bodies rot quickly, nor does the hair fall from their bodies. Despite the many who walk its paths eating fish and fatty foods without washing their hands, this returns to their benefit rather than their harm <note>The author suggests the climate prevents the typical spoilage or illness associated with poor hygiene</note>. This is especially true regarding the smell of fats; nothing of it remains in their systems, and we shall mention its spread as evidence of the health of the air and the absence of pestilence.

It is a beautiful desert settlement that chose purity on a side that has not been exploited. The length of the city from the northern side—which is the Marine side—towards the side of the Beloved—which is the Qibla <note>The direction of prayer, meaning South in the context of Upper Egypt</note>—stretching from the Small Gate to the gate known as the Street of the Potters <note>original: "Darb al-Qallaba"</note>, is one thousand five hundred cubits <term>dira'</term> <note>A cubit is an ancient unit of length based on the forearm, roughly 18-22 inches</note> by the aforementioned measurement. The measurement of the top of its wall was six thousand <note>original: "mi'at alaf"; likely a clerical error for "sittat alaf" (six thousand) as the subsequent math confirms this</note> cubits and seventy-five cubits, which equals one and a half miles plus one-twentieth of a mile.

The number of house doors through which one enters and exits is nineteen gates; one of them is plated in copper, while the others are plated in iron. If you follow the path of the gates, every dwelling has a door plated with iron to prevent anyone from entering or leaving without permission. All of its mosques and watch-stations, both inside and outside the city, excluding the Great Mosque, number one hundred and sixty-seven mosques. As for the Great Mosque <term>al-Jami'</term>, its length from the Qibla side to the northern side is one hundred and twelve cubits, and its width from East to West is seventy-one cubits. The length of the extension attached to it is seventy cubits and its width is twenty cubits. In the month of Ramadan, three thousand lamps are lit within it. There are fifty-two candlesticks, from which two thousand eight hundred lamps are lit every night. Every mosque among its mosques has a minaret. There were fifty-two primary schools <term>maktab</term> until al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah <note>The sixth Fatimid Caliph, known for his idiosyncratic and often destructive decrees</note> ordered them to be demolished in the year 403 AH <note>1012–1013 AD</note>; they were destroyed and turned into mosques in their place.

There are fifty inns and shelters, and in the year 405 AH <note>1014–1015 AD</note>, a house was established for merchants, bringing the total to fifty-nine. There are two thousand five hundred shops, and one hundred presses <note>likely for oil or wine</note>; the number of men working them varies. There are one hundred and twenty shops where bread and drinks are sold. The city contains many quarters and houses; some houses include a courtyard <term>mandar</term>, some have two courtyards, and some include a guard's room and a bakery. There are six public baths, and no ancient temples <term>barabi</term> except what the people have incorporated into their own houses.

There are five thousand looms in the workshops where clothes are made, with ten thousand workers. They produce high-quality fabrics, including the "Marzukwani" silk. Every year, a number of sealed crates <term>safat</term> are produced; of these, one thousand five hundred are general crates and one thousand are bales. Four hundred crates are marked for the Sultan’s Treasury. These include one hundred garments of gilded cloth in the "Muhattama" style, woven with gold and weighing eight hundred pounds; a single handkerchief is valued at five hundred dinars, and some garments are worth a thousand dinars. They also produce banners, cushions, large carpets, curtains, robes, loincloths, and "Saflaton" <note>a type of luxury silk fabric</note> which remains scented with expensive ambergris, and countless rings.

The area that lies West of the city contains the industry <term>al-Sina'ah</term> and the Emirate House. There are the men's baths and the open square where pigeons are fed to carry messages to the capital. In the area known as the "Dawan," the water is full and the houses are situated right at the water’s edge. Along that stretch are workshops between the city and its baths, as well as mills and stables for the Sultan in the land of "al-Saliba." The community collects water for the workshops and baths from a branch of the river. There are also the mint <note>original: "Dar al-Sikka"</note> and wood storehouses. Nearby are the lands where the plants used for the "Ruwayna" craft grow in abundance. In the "Ratha" land, water is channeled to the baths. In the "Harra" land, there are mosques, workshops, and booths for certain goods, along with engraved shops. There are also water-tanks and two prayer grounds: one for the Mu'adhdhin's inkwells <note>potentially a specialized scribal area</note> and the other for Eid prayers. Among the ancient remains is the "Hill of al-Hadi," where artifacts are found mixed in the soil like curiosities.

<summary>A detailed statistical and geographical survey of the city of Akhmim in Egypt, highlighting its healthy climate, its massive textile industry which supplied the Sultan's treasury with luxury gilded fabrics, and its urban infrastructure including mosques, schools, and baths.</summary>
<keywords>Akhmim, Upper Egypt, Fatimid industry, textiles, Al-Hakim bi-Amr Allah, urban planning, Great Mosque, Sultan's Treasury, gold weaving, historical geography</keywords>

### The Roman Sea <note>original: "al-Bahr al-Rumi"; the medieval Arabic term for the Mediterranean Sea</note>

->In this land, there are mosques, churches, and dwellings for various groups, and seas that are planted and paved near the gate, and within it are pits for ritual display.<-

->And this land is inhabited by one of the tribes of the Sudan, the Jabi and the Ad <unclear>...</unclear> due to the scarcity of the ponds.<-

---

The rising sign <term>al-Sais</term> <note>a technical term in astrology for the celestial degree ascending on the eastern horizon</note> of this city is the constellation of Pisces <note>original: "al-Hut"</note>, and its ruler is Jupiter, the Great Benefic <note>original: "al-Mushtari al-Musalla al-A'zam"</note>. Because its sign of honor is also Jupiter, the joy of its people’s souls is abundant; they are characterized by their boldness and their desire for fine food, the accumulation of wealth, and the pursuit of diverse pleasures and delights. They are known for their praises, their faith in the unseen, and their reliance on the light of the spirit. In this port, travelers board ships destined for Nia Siyun and the Karkh <note>a commercial center or suburb</note> of the Greeks <note>original: "al-Yun"</note>. They are known for their fairness in dealings and their hospitality toward strangers and travelers, providing for them in their abundance. They prefer a life of ease and play over hardship, yet they are often blamed for falling short of their duties and what is deemed proper.

<column-break/>

This city was built on the coast of Bayt Sa from the lands of the Brahmins <term>Barahin</term> <note>likely referring to a legendary class of wise kings</note>, the kings of the Copts. Great abundance used to flow there from the waters of the Nile through a vast and inhabited land, producing a variety of goods. However, the sea eventually overcame and submerged it. This shift resulted from the dominance of the "feminine" elements over Isfahan and its construction, causing the inhabitants to flee. Its lands were once a seat of royalty and high-ranking soldiers, and its inhabitants enjoyed a life of luxury and power. Ships would come to it directly from the sea. This drowning occurred shortly before the advent of Islam. Al-Mas'udi mentioned this in his book *The Meadows of Gold* <term>Muruj al-Dhahab</term> <note>A famous 10th-century history and geography work</note> when discussing the destruction of estates. We have witnessed evidence of this in our own time during excavations—artifacts recovered by diggers from locations that were once cities but have since become part of the sea. This is all by the decree of the Almighty, the All-Knowing.

<margin>
The sea itself is associated with the lands of Gemini <note>original: "al-Jawza"</note>. In the end, it was a drowning by the sea of that which was lost, and much has been said regarding what was brought forth, as we have mentioned; no other explanation is permissible.
</margin>

<margin>
In this land called the City of China <note>original: "Madinat al-Sin"</note>, merchants keep horses and trade, and they embark from here upon the paths of the Lord.
</margin>

# Description of the Fifteenth Chapter on the Islands of the Sea

> The purpose of our book is not to describe all the inhabited islands, but rather the intent is to highlight the mention of every island known and circulated by the Arabs in our book titled *The Comprehensive Guide to the Descriptions of the Walls of All Seas* <note>original: "al-Muhit bi-Sifat Judran al-Bahr"; likely referring to the coastal boundaries or shores of the seas</note>, according to our capacity and the limits of our knowledge. God is the Helper toward that which His generosity deserves.

# Map of the Island of Cyprus and its Harbors

->The length of the island is forty-five parasangs <term>farsakh</term> <note>a unit of distance roughly equal to 3-4 miles</note> and its width is twenty-two parasangs.<-
->Its beginning faces Alexandria and its end aligns with the Levant <note>original: "al-Sham"</note>.<-

# Names of its Harbors

<margin>
**Harbor descriptions in the outer compartments:**

1. The Western Harbor of Tibyun: protected from the North <note>original: "al-Awri," likely from the Greek 'Euros'</note> and South <note>original: "al-Nawtus," from the Greek 'Notos'</note> winds.
2. Butus Harbor: protected from the Cape <note>original: "al-Ras"</note> wind.
3. Qiblasi Harbor: for a ship.
4. Fadlah Harbor: for a ship, protected from the South wind and other winds.
5. The Harbor of the Seven Springs <note>original: "al-Sab' 'Uyun"</note>.
6. Wadi Layd Harbor: protected from the Boreas <note>original: "al-Bulis," the North wind</note> wind.
7. Bulat Hirz Harbor: a safe haven from the Frankish <note>original: "al-Ifranji," usually meaning the Northwest wind in this context</note> and Andalusian winds, and generally from the North wind; however, the water is distant.
8. Aqdiyunah Harbor: a large harbor protected from all winds. Between it and the Levant is a journey of a third of a day and part of a night.
9. Al-Afzakhi Harbor: protected from the South and other winds, including the wind that rises at night.
10. Qibli Harbor: protected from the wind and the Boreas.
11. Mal ba al-Nayr Harbor: protected from the Jordanian <note>original: "al-Urduni"</note> wind.
12. Harbor of Paphos <note>original: "Baf"</note>: protected from the Frankish wind from the west.
13. Al-Afrur Harbor: protected from the North wind.
14. Malih Harbor: protected from the Levant wind from the quarter of the sea.
15. The Great Butus Harbor: protected from the North wind and other winds.
16. Al-Nabat Harbor: protected from the Nabataean <note>original: "al-Nabt"</note> wind and other winds.

1. Al-Nabat Harbor: protected from the Nabataean wind and other winds.
2. Castle Harbor <note>original: "Marasa Qal'a"</note>: protected from the Cape and the Great Bear <note>original: "al-Dub," referring to the North/Polar direction</note> winds; it serves as the guard for the village.
3. Salwah Harbor: protected from the South wind; intended for large merchant ships.
4. Latiyah Harbor: a large harbor protected from the Butus and Frankish winds; it is a grand valley.

6. Al-Adi Harbor: protected from the South wind.
7. The Fortress Harbor <note>original: "al-Hisn"</note>: protected from the North and South winds.
8. Harbor of Kadha: protected from the South and Frankish winds.
9. Al-Adani Harbor: protected from the South and other winds.

## The Island of Crete

<note>original: "Iqritish"</note>
It was raided by Junada bin Abi Umayya during the time of al-Walid <note>referring to the Umayyad Caliph al-Walid I, r. 705–715 CE</note>, and he conquered parts of it through a peace treaty. It was also traversed by Ma’ruf al-Hadhani, the prayer leader for some of them. It was later invaded during the caliphate of al-Ma’mun by Abu Ja’far Umar bin Isa the Andalusian, known as "the Cretan," who captured a single fortress and continued his raids until he had conquered the island in its entirety.

## The Island of Rhodes

It was settled by Junada bin Abi Umayya al-Azdi, who took it by force. It served as a maritime capital in the year 51 of the Hijra <note>approx. 671 CE</note>. Rhodes is approximately one hundred miles long; it is a land of <term>fortifications (barakhsiya)<gloss>likely a corruption of a Greek or Persian term for defensive works or towers</gloss></term>, towers, trees, fresh water, and vineyards. He remained there for seven years. The island is one hundred miles in length and twenty miles at its widest point. Its harbor lies to the west, sheltered from all winds, with the water situated in the center.

## The Island of Sardinia

Its circumference is eight hundred miles. <unclear>Vones</unclear> breached it. Surrounding it are what we have accounted for among the islands of the <term>Mediterranean (Bahr al-Rumi)<gloss>Literally "The Sea of the Romans," the standard medieval Arabic name for the Mediterranean.</gloss></term>, totaling one hundred and sixty-two large, inhabited islands. We have enumerated these according to what was feasible within the contents of this book. We have already provided a full account of the islands in their specific chapter within our manuscript, *The Royal Book* <note>original: "al-Kitab al-Maliki"</note>. Success comes only through God.

## The Island of Ruwadah

<note>Possibly Arwad or a legendary interpretation of Rhodes’ neighbors</note>
It is currently in ruins, though in ancient times it was among the most significant islands. It was conquered by Junada bin Abi Umayya. Within it stood a city called Ruwadah, built by King Rhodes in ancient times, containing thirteen <term>talismans (tilsamat)<gloss>Magical objects or inscriptions believed to protect a city or provide supernatural benefits.</gloss></term>. There is a spring there called Abu Falys; its earth is such that whoever drinks from its water will have his soul purified so that he understands the speech of birds and wild beasts, and understands the secrets of the heart.

<margin>It is the Gulf of the Sea-Cast</margin>
## The Island of Charki

<note>likely Halki/Kharki near Rhodes</note>
It lies to the west of the island of Rhodes. It is ten miles from Rhodes and from <unclear>Zudhar</unclear>. The length of the island is fifteen miles and its width is five miles. It is eighteen miles from the mainland and contains a populated fortress.

## The Island of Tilos

It is <unclear>near</unclear> the island of Charki, ten miles away from it and thirty miles from the mainland. It faces the "Gulf of the Sea-Cast." Its length is ten miles and its width is six miles, stretching from east to west. It contains a populated fortress. We have enumerated the islands of the Mediterranean in a manner that is easy to memorize for those who wish to study them. God grants victory to those who do good.

## The Island of Malakan

<note>Possibly refers to the Nicobar or Andaman Islands</note>
It is located near <unclear>Sust</unclear> and <unclear>Kalah</unclear> in the lands of India. Its people are black and go about naked. If a human from another land falls into their hands, they <unclear>capture</unclear> him, stab him, and eat him. They are numerous in number. Their food consists of fish, bananas, and <term>coconuts (al-arjan)<gloss>a localized term for the nut of the palm.</gloss></term>. <unclear>In the season, they inhabit</unclear> thickets, marshes, and caves.

## The Island of Kadamwah

One of the islands of the <term>Zanj (East Africa/Indian Ocean)<gloss>A term used by medieval Arabs to refer to the inhabitants of the Southeast African coast and sometimes extended to distant islands in the Indian Ocean.</gloss></term>. Inhabiting it is a tribe of the Zanj called al-Busha. They wear <term>waist-wraps (al-futa)<gloss>a simple cloth wrapped around the middle.</gloss></term> and act as pirates at sea. Their weapons are swords and spears, which they use from a young age. The island is seven miles long and contains various types of <unclear>clothing</unclear> and fabrics. They claim that there are <note>original: "Majus"</note> sorcerers on the backs of the men <note>This may be a distorted description of ritual scarring or tattoos common in the region.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the geographical survey, moving from the Mediterranean islands of the previous page (Crete, Rhodes, Sardinia) to the islands of the Indian Ocean and the African coast, focusing on their natural resources, inhabitants, and unique customs.</meta>

# The Island of al-Azala <note>original: "al-Azala," possibly referring to an island near the East African coast or a transcription error for a known location</note> ◦
Its inhabitants are Black people. In particular, it contains <term>al-qurandul<gloss>possibly cloves or a specific aromatic root</gloss></term>, which they cook for food in the land of Gharim. It also contains <term>al-sabija<gloss>a type of dark gemstone or jet</gloss></term> and the "stone of the Tuaregs." There are monkeys there as large as mountains that inhabit the wilderness of this island.

# The Island of Barkaw Az, one of the Islands of the Indian Sea ◦
It lies near India and is inhabited by sailors. Among its strongholds are the Fortress of the Christians, the Fortress of Crystal <note>original: "al-Ballur"</note>, and the Fortress of the Arabs. In its interior are the Fortress of Usr and the Fortress of al-Qasm. Along the <term>shores (al-hazu)<gloss>referring to the coastal periphery</gloss></term> are the Fortress of Masru, the Fortress of Karjan, and the Fortress of Abu Sinan. These serve as <term>anchorages (masabir)<gloss>protected maritime stations</gloss></term> against the rugged terrain. It contains several districts where ships are safe and secure, with abundant water and places of assembly <note>original: "makhtab," likely referring to places for public address or Friday prayers</note>. Its people are Abyssinians <note>original: "Abasibnah," likely a corruption of "al-Habasha"</note>. It possesses many pearl-diving sites, and its length is eighteen <term>farsakhs<gloss>a medieval unit of distance, roughly 3 to 4 miles or 5 to 6 kilometers</gloss></term>. ◦

# The Island of Sarandib <note>Modern-day Sri Lanka</note> ◦
It is situated on the Equator and contains mountainous cities. It has two kings and is located in the Sea of Harkand <note>The medieval Arabic name for the waters around Sri Lanka and the Bay of Bengal</note>. Its inhabitants include the tribe of Bani Kudama. Within it is Mount al-Rahun <note>Adam's Peak</note>, upon which Adam—prayers of God be upon him—descended; his footprint is preserved in the stone. Water has partially covered the footprint. From Sarandib, one can observe <unclear>the sky/horizon</unclear> until it is visible, and around it are red fish, as red as blood, <unclear>from which people perish</unclear>. 

Sarandib is the source of Indian agarwood <note>original: "al-ʿud al-hindi"</note>, the likes of which cannot be found elsewhere, as well as mines of gold, and red, yellow, and blue rubies, and diamond mines, and things of <unclear>al-mahu</unclear>. It contains a stone called "al-Qasuri" that grows within camphor trees, and on its ground, one finds musk. In the city of Marand, there is an idol said to be "the Sign, Daughter of <unclear>Hadath</unclear>," fashioned in the likeness of the moon. Regarding the gold of Marand, it is mentioned in their records that it yields two hundred <unclear>hara</unclear> of gold, and by day four hundred pounds. There is no gold on the face of the earth larger than the "Stone of Sarandib." 

Its people <unclear>forgo wine</unclear>. Its stages and distances are measured by hand-spans and footsteps for security. After a period of peace, it fell into ruin, but the city of Mandur Fayr became known as its successor. It is guarded by elephants. Regarding the customs of the kings of Sarandib: when a king dies, everyone present at the royal court—loved ones and others among the king's people—shave <insert>their heads</insert>. They carry the deceased king on a cart, his head hanging down toward the ground, while a woman sweeps dust onto his hair. A crier calls out: "O people, look at the finery and splendor of the world! This command was for the king, but now the king cannot defend himself from anything."

# The Island of Socotra ◦
Its length is eighty *farsakhs*, and it contains eight cities. Its inhabitants are Christians of the Nestorian rite. They suffer from <unclear>extreme heat</unclear>. Their <unclear>livestock</unclear> are kept with fodder. It is a land of difficult winds, and it is inhabited by those who intercept ships as pirates. <unclear>Lizards</unclear> are not found in Socotra on some of its sides. In a village <unclear>exposed to the wind</unclear>, its inheritance is called "Mahko."

# The Islands of al-Mubahat ◦
They are ruled by a woman. People are carried there by the <unclear>winds</unclear> <unclear>like sweeping fire</unclear>. The <unclear>deposed king</unclear> is called <unclear>by his great title</unclear>. <unclear>Black</unclear> <unclear>birds</unclear> are found there. They are among the most beautiful of people, wearing <unclear>Siyuwi</unclear> clothes, both large and <unclear>patterned</unclear>. The King wears a cloak of <unclear>fine fabric</unclear> with <unclear>her name</unclear> upon it.

# The Island of al-Zanj <note>The East African coast/Zanzibar region</note> ◦
A great and large island. It contains several kings from the kings of India <note>likely referring to the Indian cultural and trade influence or a medieval geographic grouping</note>, and its rivers are <unclear>in their natural state</unclear>.

<summary>This page describes various islands of the Indian Ocean and East Africa, highlighting Sarandib (Sri Lanka) for its gems and the ritualistic funeral of its kings, and Socotra for its Nestorian Christian population.</summary>

...and its bark is used for protection in war. The camphor tree <note>original: "shajarat al-kafur"</note> is so large that it provides shade for a hundred men. There is also the *Luqm* tree, which bears a fruit resembling carob <note>original: "al-kharnub"</note>; it is among the foods that are not eaten. Its roots are like those of the <term>Maqam Maryam<gloss>literally "the station of Mary," likely referring to the Cyclamen plant or "Sowbread," used in medieval medicine</gloss></term>. Among these areas are numerous islands and many buffalo. There are pests and gnats throughout **the Island of al-Dasiyah** <note>possibly a corruption of a name for an island in the Andaman or Nicobar chain</note>.

Its inhabitants are <term>Zanj<gloss>a historical term used by medieval Arabic writers to refer to the peoples of the East African coast and sometimes other dark-skinned peoples of the Indian Ocean</gloss></term> with "peppery" [tightly curled] hair. If they capture Arabs, they eat them, being as ravenous for human flesh as rabid dogs. They spare only the women. Some of them have elongated faces and swellings, and are deformed in appearance. 

**The Island of Barkanus** <note>possibly related to the Latin "Vulcanus," suggesting a volcanic island</note>
Its people are destitute and naked. Neither the men nor the women cover themselves, except for their private parts which they hide with tree leaves. From some of these leaves, they weave fabrics. Resources in their lands are plentiful. [The inhabitants] block those ships in their harbors, and the Arabs then pursue them with various types of iron tools. Some of them carry large shields made of hides. Ptolemy <note>original: "Batlamus," referring to the 2nd-century geographer Claudius Ptolemy</note> estimated that in the <term>Green Sea<gloss>a medieval Arabic term for the Indian Ocean</gloss></term> there are twenty-seven thousand islands, both inhabited and uninhabited. 

**These seas have drainage points** for when they surge and overflow. The total area of their surfaces and the measurement of their distances in <term>farsakhs<gloss>a unit of distance roughly equal to 3-4 miles</gloss></term> were recorded by the people of the <term>Ahwaz<gloss>referring to the marshy regions of Khuzestan/Southern Iraq</gloss></term> regions. The Arabs call these measurements the *Dahad*. The extent of the fertile regions has been calculated along the northern routes so that they may be known, measured, and organized for [human] understanding, by the will of God and His help. 

## Chapter Sixteen: On the Shapes of the Mountains within the Inlets
->Specifically, the Inlets of the Land of the Romans<- <note>original: "Bilad al-Rum," referring to the Byzantine Empire or the broader Mediterranean European coast</note>

->The Small Inlet of Tarhana<-
<gloss>Its beginning is one thousand eight hundred miles from the sea, and its width is one hundred miles. Ptolemy mentioned that it is inhabited by the "Berman," who are masters of theft and wealth.</gloss>

->The Great Inlet of Tarhana<-
<gloss>Its length from the sea is two thousand five hundred miles, and its width is three hundred miles. It is inhabited by a people of the tongue mentioned in the Book of Ptolemy—the Franks—living on an inhabited island.</gloss>

->The Inlet of Kadu<-
<gloss>Its length from the sea is two thousand seven hundred miles, and its width is eight hundred miles. The width of the basin of this inlet is eight hundred miles across.</gloss>

->The Inlet of Mulayma<-
<gloss>Its length from the sea is eighty-eight thousand miles, and its width is one hundred and twenty miles. It empties into the west of the Sea of "Al-Ma," the Dead Sea.</gloss>

->The Inlet of Maya Taywa<-
<gloss>The length of this inlet from the sea is two thousand seven hundred and fifty-eight miles, and its width is one hundred and ten miles. Its exit is from the rising of the sea and its mouth is in the Sea of Alman...</gloss>

In the middle of this bay <note>original: "al-jawn," referring to a gulf or deep inlet</note> is a flourishing fort called Satluwa <note>likely Stilo in Calabria, Italy</note>, and it is five miles out into the sea. To the west of it is a valley through which <term>Salamiyyat<gloss>likely a type of Byzantine vessel, such as the *chelandion*</gloss></term> can travel freely due to its width. On its side is a flourishing fort, though partially in ruins. After it to the west is **the Bay of Fulughrah** <note>possibly referring to Phlegra or a similar coastal site in Southern Italy</note>; its length is forty miles and the width of its entrance is ten miles. Its entrance runs from north to south. In its final third is a small, round island containing a flourishing fort called Mahalla. Between the island and the mainland is less than a mile, and at its end is a fort called Bulughar.

To the north of it is **the Bay of Ibri** <note>possibly the Gulf of Evros or a phonetic rendering of a local name</note>; its length is <unclear>    </unclear> miles and its width at the entrance is four miles. Its entrance runs from west to east. To the north of it is **the Bay of Azmufah**, which is thirty miles long and ten miles at its widest point. In its recess <note>original: "lughra," likely meaning the innermost part of the bay</note> is a flourishing fort called Azmirnah <note>original: "Azmirnah," referring to Smyrna, modern-day Izmir</note>; between it and the mainland are three miles. At its entrance is a small, uninhabited island known as the Island of Jirjin. On its southern side is a fort called Qalunumti <note>likely Clazomenae</note> and on the northern side is a fort called Fawqah <note>likely Phocaea</note>. Its entrance runs from west to east.

Within it is a flourishing, inhabited island called **Khata al-Uns** <note>likely the island of Chios</note>; it is twenty miles long and seven miles wide. Its entrance runs from the west to the east <note>original: "al-marw," likely a scribal error for "al-mashriq" (the east)</note>. It contains a flourishing, inhabited fort called Nafusa. To the north of it is **the Bay of Abtah**, which is ten miles long and four miles wide at the entrance. Its entrance runs from the south to the west. At its entrance is a small island called Barsuwa. To the north of it is **the Bay of Satr Taklalah**, which is forty miles long, and the width of its entrance from west to east is twenty miles. In it is an uninhabited island called Aristas <note>possibly Aristera</note>, where Satr Taklalah is located. Between Aristas and the mainland is a small uninhabited island.

To the west is **the Gate of the Strait** <note>original: "Bab al-Khalij," usually referring to the Dardanelles or the Bosphorus</note>. At its entrance are two small, uninhabited islands called Mafriyah. On its eastern side is a flourishing fort called Abadh <note>likely Abydos</note>. The entrance of the Gate of the Strait runs from south to north. The narrowest point in the Gate of the Strait is half a mile wide.

To the west of it is **the Bay of Fardamah**, thirty miles long and six miles wide; its entrance runs from south to north. On its eastern side is a flourishing fort called Afrashar, and on the western side is a flourishing fort called Abusar. To the west of it is **the Bay of Barbura**, ten miles long and four miles wide. Its entrance runs from south to north. On its eastern side by the sea is a flourishing fort called Barbun, and to its west is a flourishing fort belonging to the <term>Silva<gloss>possibly referring to a forest region or a specific Latin-derived place name</gloss></term>. To the west of it is a harbor called al-Maksatun. To the west of that is a fort on the sea called Satr Lanah. To the west of that is a fort called Utru <note>likely Otranto</note>; it sits upon a rock detached from the mainland. The sea enters these places between the fort until it covers the path, so when the sea is rough, no one is able to enter it.

To the west of it is **the Bay of Astunu Metre**, thirty miles long and ten miles wide. It is within the territory of the Sicilians <note>original: "al-Saqaliyyah"</note>. Its entrance runs from south to north. To the west of it is **the Bay of Armabliyah**, which is twenty miles long and twenty miles wide. In its middle, on the eastern side, is an island known as the Island of the Deep <note>original: "al-Lajj"</note>, which is uninhabited. After the bay and the circuit of Chios <note>original: "Hiyusa"</note>, there is a place on the sea, than which there is none greater in the lands of Christendom <note>original: "bilad al-nisraniyah"</note>, called Malastar. 

To the west of it is **the Bay of Salonica** <note>original: "al-Lawnuqiyah"</note>, which is fifty miles long and twenty miles wide. In its recess is a fort on the sea called Salunqiyah <note>original: "Salunqiyah," Thessaloniki</note>. At its entrance is a flourishing fort on the sea called Findu. Within this bay’s recess on the eastern side is a small bay called Fil. To its east is a fort on the sea called Kabrash. To the south of it is **the Bay of Damatribadah** <note>likely the Gulf of Demetrias</note>.

**The Bay of Slo-fante** <note>possibly Squillace or a similar phonetic rendering of a Southern European coastal name</note> and from it a mile inland, its length is thirteen miles <note>original: "amthal," meaning "measures" or "units," here used for miles</note>. In its center is an island known as the **Island of the Monk**. At the end of the bay is a fortress called Dhimtar, in a land that is uninhabited. Outside the bay is a place known as **Al-Salib** <note>The Cross</note>. Following it to the south and west is:

**The Bay of Lasadhurs**
Its length is sixteen miles and its width is ten miles. This bay is expansive, and in its middle, on one side, is the fortress of Hurma reaching to the fortress of Ratlanus. To the east of this bay is an uninhabited island called Petro Swa. At a third of the way into its entrance is an island called **The Island of Heat** <note>original: "Jazirat al-Hamiya"</note>, and to the west of it is an island called Petro. Its entrance runs from north to south and west. Following it, between the east and the south, is:

**The Bay of Fadlo Faris**
At the edge of this bay is the **Port of the Chain** <note>original: "Marsa al-Silsila"</note> and an island called Fariqa. Its length is forty miles and its width is six miles. At its furthest point is a flourishing fortress called the Fortress of 'Ayira. Following it to the south and west is:

**The Bay of Ratlanus**
Its length is one mile and its width is ten miles. At its entrance is a small uninhabited island called Batlanus. Outside of it, within the bay, is a small uninhabited island called Tafnisa. To its west is an island called **New Petro**. North of the border of Petro is a rock two miles long and half a mile wide where ships anchor. From the "New Port" in that other bay is an uninhabited island called Fukash. To its west, inside the bay, is another uninhabited island. To the north of the bay is an inlet called the **Bay of Salama** <note>Bay of Safety</note>; its length is three miles and its width is one mile. Following it to the west is:

**The Bay of Qur-lasha**
Its length is eight miles and its width is ten miles. Its entrance runs from east to west. Outside it is a small uninhabited island called Quriya. To the west of it are four islands. Within the bay is a flourishing fortress called Quriya; between it and the sea is a distance of four miles. After this bay, between the west and the south, is a fortress called Milasir, three miles from the sea's edge. Following it to the south and west is:

**The Bay of Anila**
Its length is ten miles and its width is ten miles. At its furthest point is a flourishing fortress called Anila, which is near the Arabs. At its entrance is an island known as the **Island of the Pines**. The sea to the south and west is inhabited by the **Slavs** <note>original: "Al-Saqaliba," referring to Slavic peoples who settled Byzantine coastal areas</note>; it is called Rajiqa <note>possibly Ragusa, modern-day Dubrovnik</note>. Between the south and Rajiqa to the west is the Fortress of Karsa. To its south is the Fortress of Manusha. Also to the south of Manusha is a place called Milabri.

**Description of the Sea Route from Constantinople to Isqala** <note>original: "Isqala" refers to a port, pier, or "scale"</note>
Following it to the west is **The Bay of Yalis**; its length is three miles and its width is twenty miles. It is a land inhabited by the **Slavs** and is known as Arza. The entrance of this bay lies between the south and the east. Following it to the west is:

**The Bay of Qustata**
Its length is twenty-five miles and its width is twenty-five miles. At its end is a fortress called Malata <note>possibly Malatya or a coastal namesake</note>. Between it and the sea is a landmark known as a headland <note>original: "ras"</note>, upon which is a fortress called Munabbih. To the west is an island called Ward-mula. Further west is a flourishing fortress on the sea called Qaruna. Its entrance runs from the south to the west. Following it to the west is:

**The Bay of Mashuna**
Its length is twenty miles and the width of its entrance is twenty-five miles. In it is a flourishing fortress called Mashuna. In its center is an island called Al-Marudh, which contains a harbor where the winds grow still. Following it to the west is:

**The Bay of Arqala** <note>possibly Heraclea</note>
Its length is five miles and the width of its entrance is ten miles. In its center is an uninhabited island called Arqala. Following it to the west is:

**The Bay of Butrus** <note>Bay of Peter</note>
Its furthest point reaches to Al-Quriya. To the south of it is a flourishing fortress called Ratous. The length of this bay is seventy-five miles and the width of its entrance is twenty-five miles. In its center is an uninhabited island known as the **Island of the Crows** <note>original: "Jazirat al-Kurab"</note>. There are other uninhabited islands there. From the mountains to the west is the limit of the territory inhabited by the **Slavs**. The entrance of the bay runs from west to east.

To the east of the Bay of Qalata is a small fortress called **Fard Maula**, and to its west is a station with a populated fortress on the sea called **Qazuna**. # Chapter Seventeen: On the Description of Lakes

The largest lake on the face of the earth is the one from which the Nile flows, called **Al-Batiha** <term>The Great Marsh or Basin</term>, located on the equator. It is the source and the drainage of the Nile. Its length and width are <unclear>illegible</unclear>. From it rises a mountain that is never free of snow, winter or summer. Most of the <term>Copts</term> <note>native Egyptian Christians</note> claim that when the sun is directly over that mountain in the summer, the Nile begins to rise and surge in its basin. Opinions regarding the increase and decrease of the Nile's flow differ greatly. We have presented here the most plausible explanations for the listener, conveying the meaning according to our capacity and the best of our ability. Success is through God; we ask Him for protection from error and oversight.

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion of maritime geography, moving from the Mediterranean and Byzantine frontiers to the inland hydrology of Africa. This lake is called the **Batiha** <note>original: "al-Batiha," a term used for a wide, shallow marshy lake or swamp</note>, and it lies upon the equator. In it is a mountain which snow never leaves, neither in summer nor in winter. A group of scholars claims that the flooding of the Nile comes from the torrents of this mountain in the summer. **Ptolemy** <note>the 2nd-century Alexandrian geographer whose work heavily influenced medieval Islamic cartography</note> called it the **Mountain of the Moon** <note>original: "Jabal al-Qamar"</note>. Beyond it are the dwellings of the **Zanj** <term>Zanj</term> <note>a medieval Arabic term referring to the peoples and regions of the southeastern African coast</note> and twelve... <unclear>they are eight haj</unclear> <note>the text here appears to be a corrupted numerical notation or fractional measurement</note>.

(An illustration depicting the Mountain of the Moon as a stylized red, scaled peak against a dark blue background.)

This **Batiha** is called the **Eastern** one. Five rivers flow into it from the **Mountain of the Moon**, and three rivers flow out from it into the **Great Batiha**. And from the third **Batiha**...

This lake is called the **Western Batiha**. Six rivers flow from it into the **Greatest Batiha**, and five rivers flow into it.

(A diagram of two circular lakes connected by intersecting lines representing the complex network of rivers feeding the Nile.)

**Al-Qarun** <note>often identified with Lake Qarun in the Faiyum, though here placed in a more southern context</note> is located near the city of **Marinda** in the land of the **Zanj**. The Nile of Egypt has its origin there. In the sand-hills <note>original: "qawz," referring to high, shifting dunes</note> of the **Zanj** are terrifying wildernesses, soft sands, and dunes near the city of **Dongola** <note>a major city and capital of the Nubian kingdom of Makuria</note>. It is among the largest and greatest of the marsh-lakes.

This marsh-lake is called the **Zanj Batiha**, and **Ptolemy** named it **Niphon**. It begins and flows into it, and from it emerge the **Sumar** crocodiles <note>original: "al-sumar," possibly a specific regional name for a variety of crocodile or aquatic lizard</note>. A great river flows out from it, cutting through the land until it appears in the **Nubian land**, where it joins the Nile.

(An illustration of a reddish-brown crocodile or lizard-like creature on a dark blue ground, representing the fauna of the Upper Nile.)

<margin>From it and escaping into</margin>
Lake Maskana in the <term>Land of the Romans</term> <note>Arabic: "Bilad al-Rum," referring to the Byzantine Empire.</note>
The Salt-works Lake in that land, its length is four <term>parasangs</term> <note>A "farsakh" or parasang is a unit of distance approximately equal to 3 to 4 miles (5–6 kilometers).</note> and its width
is ten; its water is rough and contains no fish,
nor any beast, except one resembling a bride. From it comes the light of industry.
The River Jordan has a lake in its center <note>The Sea of Galilee.</note> which neither increases nor decreases.
Its length during its agitation is like a night; no animal enters it.
It is known for its <term>thermal properties</term> <note>Arabic: "al-hamma."</note> and is used extensively in bathhouses.
Lake Apamea, into which the <term>Inverted River</term> <note>The Orontes River (Nahr al-Maqlub), so named because it flows from south to north, contrary to the other major rivers of the region.</note> flows,
and from there it pours toward Antioch.
Lake Munyat Ta'ma, into which its water flows and empties,
and [the traveler] crosses it.

<column-break/>

Lake Azmura in the Land of the Romans.
Lake of Al-Mastaghbarish, which empties.
Lake Khalat <note>Modern-day Lake Van in Turkey; Ahlat (Khalat) was a major city on its northern shore.</note>
Its length is half of a tenth of a parasang, and its water is salty.
A lake in the <term>Maghreb</term> <note>The Islamic West, referring to North Africa or al-Andalus.</note> nearby
which contains many shells.
Lake Tiberias <note>The Sea of Galilee.</note> and Siniya; its water
cools quickly and its depth is solid.
Buildings stand upon it.
Lake Dunir, its taste is salty, and there emerges toward it
a canal.
<margin>The Qirghiz</margin>
A lake, its depth at...
Lake of the Bani Shayban, known for its coldness and blackness.
Marshes among the coastal people where they flow; in it is the... wheat.
A lake near Siniya, which contains seven [features].
Lake Nama from Bukhara
and al-Barma, its length is forty parasangs.

<column-break/>

Lake Mabliyun in the Land of the Romans.
Lake Urjanar, which empties into...
The Lake of Armenia, called Kuwan in Adranjan.
It is salty and contains neither fish nor beasts. There is a language there...
ten canals from the shepherds and the oases, salty...
It is salty, containing no animals, nor is any living thing known to survive in it;
rather, when it dies, it sinks. In that region are a people who do not know
the Black people, though they have white faces
and beautiful forms. Chroniclers claim they are the companions of
the Prophet Jonah, peace be upon him, and no...
none of them have remained silent.
Another lake, Dunsiyya in Saharand.
<margin>And where they dwelt</margin>
It is salty, and upon it are the Arad, the tracks of the Arabs.
On the right, it does not leave; it emerges from the face of the earth.
Lake Samatur in the Land of the Romans.
Lake Maskalon in the Land of the Romans.
It emerges from a mountain upon which are several rivers that gather
into it, a river of high elevation, a journey of a year...
A lake seen at the edge of the water, its length is five parasangs.

---

Chroniclers claim that the Lake of the Nile, which God Almighty sent down at the request of the priests for what they had expended upon it, is named for its "bride" and its blessings; it is sevenfold and divided into twelve paths. Some are for the believer, for the believer spends his wealth on righteousness and charity, while the disbeliever is deprived of it in this world. The believer, in some of his days, finds it spread out for him, and it was said: "I have honored you with wealth and made you mighty in followers." The mouth of the Nile at that hour was empty. The Lake of the Nile rose a degree, and its waves entered from the mounds in a sequence; its cultivation increased. Its construction was on the edge of its sea, like a window overlooking the land from an opening, and that was three hundred and fifty years before the Prophet of Islam.

# Mountain of the Moon

->**The Illustration of the Nile**<-

This is an illustration showing where the rivers rise in the Mountain of the Moon <note>Arabic: "Jabal al-Qamar." Historically, this was believed by medieval geographers—following Ptolemaic tradition—to be the source of the Nile in East Africa.</note>. Six rivers emerge from its source and gather into two <term>basins</term> <note>Arabic: "batiha," referring to a shallow marsh, lagoon, or basin where river water spreads out.</note>. From each of these two basins, three rivers emerge, and they all meet in a single basin in the center of the <term>First Climate</term> <note>The "Climes" (Iqlim) were seven latitudinal zones used by ancient and medieval geographers to organize the known world based on daylight hours.</note>. A single river then emerges from it, which flows into the Roman Sea <note>The Mediterranean Sea.</note> and a lake called Qubtuwa. The remainder of the water that descends from the Mountains of the <term>Wandat</term> <note>Likely referring to the "Vandals" or a specific mountain range associated with them in medieval lore.</note> passes through the region of Ethiopia and between <term>al-Andalus</term> <note>Usually referring to Islamic Iberia; its mention here reflects a medieval geographical conception of the Nile's reach or a confusion with the "Nile of the Negroes" (the Niger River) which was thought to flow west.</note>. It then shifts from its alignment with the <term>Equator</term> <note>Arabic: "Khatt al-Istiwā'."</note>, veering north until it pours into the Salt Sea. 

We have measured the variations at the edge of the land of <unclear>Kaza</unclear>, the stations of Qalat, the Land of the Blacks <note>Arabic: "Bilad al-Sudan."</note>, and the lands of lions, wild beasts, and others within the Land of <term>Qumar</term> <note>Likely a variant of "Qamar" (Moon) or a reference to Madagascar or the Khmer Empire, often conflated in medieval accounts of the southern reaches of the world.</note>, the true nature of which no one knows but God Almighty. The waters of the Nile flow from its south to its north as if it were a salt river, just as the waters of the Nile are; however, its volume increases primarily from the abundance of rainwater and the rise of the <insert>Sudar</insert> wall. It used to flow at times intended for the irrigation of the lands, overseen in the city of Egypt by the <term>Judge of Giza</term> <note>The "Qadi of Giza" was historically involved in the administration of the Nile's irrigation and the monitoring of the Nilometer.</note>—so know this.

---

<margin>
This river begins from below the Equator.
</margin>

<margin>
The extent of this Eastern Sea is seven degrees; its celestial measure is two hundred and eighty-four miles.
</margin>
